wall, went eight paces past it alone at number fifty-eight, came off a join before it had her
and said so as she went; **Mace has left her name where it was** (*Said so as she went*). Read
the *why.* Called the step in the middle of ten wards for the four-knot string. **Has told her
mother.**

**Perrin Cade.** Proposed the four-dram loan. **380 kilos for thirty counts, cut in the stone;
a place in his Hall's second team from the new year.** Carried a litter, an old man in a
chair, and a boy out of a shaft by one arm. Two to the bar with Brask. **Two ribs sprung, not
broken;** strapped again. Said that he does mind, all of it, into a bar.

**Kellan Renn.** **Has taken the Halden yard** (Tuesdays and Thursdays, nothing owed, his own
cuff; Birch: "They've not got worse"). On Seven's book "as a blade who's told where he's
wanted." Cut a clean rung in the yard on a catcher's word that was by the book and wrong, and
watched it cook a bank. Cut the eleventh knot on Pru's word. Read out his mother's wire.

**Noa Bexley.** Sat the School's mast and called the mark and then the drum. Went to the
superseded stacks. Read the travelling mast on the 5th: two cliffs of 0.26 and a notch eleven
seconds after the second, **five minutes before the lamps jumped.** His page now reads *Not yet
weighed* and under it *Not one thing,* with the four-month mistake left in and ruled through.
Three columns (section 5); Adlam is second; the Chair has sent them to the Charter Board.

**Jude Talley.** Seven's Kill by name. Asked aloud whether the sealed reserve or the red bags
might be used; was refused; was thanked for asking. Cut the tenth knot and a husk. **7.8 drams
by name in one autumn.** Proposed a dram to Stopgate's wardens. Will stand at Saltmere if
asked by name, and was.

**Pru Finch.** A catcher with a sheet of her own. Folded a ward under a loaded cord twice
(nipped to the first joint the first time; twelve minutes). Had the gap on the night. Caught
the eleventh knot over a planked grating with a warden for a corner; sealed at 48; **wrote her
own red tag.** Told a Fire *not yet* twice. Rhea Sorn underlined the cord on her card. Asked
to the night van with Kiva and said yes "so quickly that I bit my tongue."

**Selka Dray.** In an infirmary chair, the leg in a board; on her feet "by the third month."
**Called the count on the 5th, on three terms** (a needle on Kiva; Marten carries the chair;
written that she objected twice and has not changed her mind). Her mast rule now goes to the
National Measure under seal. "I'd have seated you on that. I'd not have seated you on the
other." Not a friend. Means to beat Kiva in a lane at Saltmere.

**Lowen Brask.** Felt the post in the dark and said what wanted saying at the gap. Called the
slack for Pru. Named the string as a hold with nobody keeping it. Two to the bar.

**Rhea Sorn.** Refused to have the three-door offered on her card on the 2nd ("It's too
dear"). Ruled a sixth column, *Before,* at the left of her slate. Signed on the 5th after
hearing the price from the healer and the bad answer from Kiva. Cut the eighth knot, the seventh and the twelfth,
and stood to cut the last string. Wrote to the board; **the brackets came off Team Seven and
Old Four on the 14th.** Her card is section 4.

**Hollis Vane.** Foreman of the response. Gave the post three days. Found the lane sheet at two
in the morning. Third direction in the ledger, and the cost after. Moved, by his own account,
"a thing" in the watch; the River Flats made the motion.

**Ysra Crest.** Lent the hearing room. "Nobody in this yard will ask." A witness on the 5th.
Sealed and opened Mara's paper. Posted "seated." Sent the three sheets to the Charter Board.

**Others.** **Examiner Rusk:** wrong about the firebreak, said so in the street, took off his
cuff guards, handed over the trunk, pulled a handle and read the odd cells. **Mr. Adlam:**
astonished a sixth time; saw twenty-four dead needles lift at *back.* **Foreman Birch:** lent
his yard, his slate and his string. **Kellan's mother** (signs "A. Renn, grader"): the
step glass; found the cell that had fallen back in; knew a starved hand. **Pike:** the
handoff list; "Who called that?"; will have Kiva as his second. **Greta Stroud:** warned two
floors; took the end of the wall when Anwen was fetched. **Doran Mace:** the porch.
**Halloran:** twenty-four jars and no more. **Healer Aske:** two cards; would not round the
price down. **Wace, Hendry Moss, Yarrow:** the corner. **Wilf Marten:** the chair. **Nairn:**
walking, his stick thrown away, at Rhea Sorn's side for the last string. **Bettan:** closed it.

## 3. The go

**Three doors, the 5th of the tenth month, the Old Yard.** Price whole, as before: 0.02 (ward,
one tap) + 0.26 + 0.02 (bound) + 0.26 + 0.03 (stance) = **0.59**, from the team's line. Read
by Noa on the Circuit's travelling mast under the shed lamp: "Two cliffs. Twenty-six, and
twenty-six. Nothing between but the bound."

| Step | What the page shows |
|---|---|
| Step first | Disk cold. No white. Stance tried four paces east of the post: "a room underneath," eight feet down, bricked, keeping the count. **Not clean; no go there.** Tried four paces north of the lid, mid-lane: "only ground." A cross scraped with her heel. |
| The turn | "The mark's turned! It isn't east! East's the drain! I'm carrying it north." Said back by Brask and by Wace. |
| Catch | Standing on the half-dragged iron lid. Ward low at the right hip, bowl turned the other way (curve to the east, open side north), first two fingers clear. Under the arm on Dray's *in,* called a hair early as promised. |
| Carry | The haul of ten knots turns a quarter round in the ward and throws her north. Fold from the far edge to a penny. First gap: the work comes, the count does not. Left hand cold to the wrist. |
| Plant | Second gap "came out of a place that had been emptied in the Pit, and had only half filled"; she feels it pass the wrist and the elbow. Right foot on the cross. "Planted! Six!" |
| Hand off | At about nine: "Wace. The plank. Your end." She leans the corner from her heel to the plank and **shuts with nothing in the doorway.** |
| After | With nothing open she calls fives for three still-doors for the best part of three hundred counts, and calls *back* on a slack, one whole slack after *Still.* |

Changed conditions against the Pit: at night; on a longer arm and ten knots, not three posts;
the count called by a rival from a chair; the throw turned across the haul and not along it;
the corner given away at nine and not held for ten; the release called minutes later.

Aske's price, said before: "I think it goes to the elbow… I think so. I've no case to think it
from… It may be that she keeps a hand that grips and doesn't feel." After: "I've a case now.
One… I don't know that it would a fourth time; I think it took a deal more than it took in the
Pit, and returned it slower."

## 4. Records and rulings

**Mara's sealed paper** (sealed by the Chair on the 28th; opened in the street on the 3rd):
*If it is the drain: drum twenty-eight, on Monday the third of next month, with two dry drums
between. If it is the copper or the ash: nothing, and I am wrong. M. Rowan, Grade Two.*

**Mara's half sheet of the 2nd** (the assumption, written "so that it can be wrong in front of
everybody"): *That the water moves because the pumps move it. Stop the pumps and the main lies
still. Then a bank may be quenched and filled fresh, and it will keep the time that it is
given.* It was wrong within the hour.

**Aske's cards.** The 30th: single doors and dark hands from that morning; *A crossing, one, in
seven days, as before. The longer thing: not on my card. If somebody with a slate means to
direct it, he sends for me and I say the price to his face, before, with the candidate
listening.* The 10th of the tenth month: nothing lit until the new year.

**The *Standard Lane Manual,* eleventh edition** (withdrawn 2603; the superseded stacks): six
ruled columns in print and a seventh, *The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road
isn't straight,* with the junior archivist's pencil beside it. A plate shows two rows of posts
on a curve, the last of the south row a pace out with a bar across its top. The School "set
the posts fair" in 2390 (eleven days, in the bursar's accounts) and could not shift the last.
"It kept the why and lost the what."

**The Home Line's lane sheet** (a copy in the record hand of something older; from the chest
under the bearer's box; read by the Reader on the 3rd):
*The lane with the crooked posts. At Meridian, under the south wall, on the line of the hill
drain. / What: nine posts, set as the drain runs, which is not straight. The ninth is the
sluice post. It stands out from the wall for the bar. / How: run it dark, from the sheds. Call
the turn before the eighth, aloud, and say who has the bar. Two to the bar. Nine turns shuts
the hill from the low streets. / Went wrong: ran it straight and silent, and met the ninth.
Also: two came to the bar, and nobody had said which two. / Cost: a collarbone. A cellar in the
Hythe, the night that nobody had said. / Second try: called it, and said which two. Shut in
nine turns. / Why: because the road isn't straight. S.V.*
Its seven columns, as the page now names them: the lane's name and place; *What; How; Went
wrong; Cost; Second try; Why.* The manual's page has the same seven. Nobody on the page knows
whose initials those are.

**The tenth edition's proof** (the 16th): the lane restored in seven columns. Kiva's pencil, in
the fourth column, under *Went wrong:* *2680. The mark was called east, since east had served once before. East was
the drain. The step was tried first, and it said so.* She passes over the fifth (*Cost,* "where the collarbone was"). In the sixth,
under *Second try:* *Called north, aloud,
before. Said back by two. Three others had the corner after six. Two had the bar. One had the
count, from a chair. K.R.* She did not touch the seventh and did not enter the cost.

**The Works' drain plan.** Its blue line across the School's bottom yard is "recopied from the
worn sheet, 2553," straight. The main in fact turns west inside the gate.

**Rhea Sorn's slate** now has six columns: *Before. Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.* The
entry of the 5th: *Before: the left hand, to the elbow. Her healer does not know that it
returns. Offered. Not asked.*

**Vane's ledger,** *Directions to the Unplaced,* fourth entry: *Direction to K. Rowan, catcher,
T7, attached, under R. Sorn's hand. Three doors in sequence, hand, Ember, still, declared.
Cost, as stated before by her healer: the left hand, to the elbow; he does not know that it
returns. Reason: an arm of a string of ten in the Stopgate sluice, driving the Ferren main, and
a bank that cannot be filled while it drives. No other hand on this hill takes it off the
water. Offered by the candidate. Not asked. The count is S. Dray's. The corner after six is L.
Brask's, or Wace's.* **After:** *the left arm, without feeling to a hand above the elbow. Four
days. Returned to where it was. The first two fingers as before.* And, where E.K. wrote the
other: ***Rowan, K. Entered on the Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as before.***

**The team book's *why,*** read by Anwen on the line: *Wednesday the 5th. Three-door. For: to
take an arm off the Ferren main, so that a gate can be shut and a bank filled. Likely cost:
her left hand, to the elbow, and perhaps kept.*

**The bearer's book,** fourth column, the 10th (witnessed by Vane): *Cold. Wednesday the 5th,
in the Old Yard, about midnight. After three doors in sequence, under direction, with an arm of
a string taken off a drain, and carried, and planted, and given to three others. Cold before,
during and after. / I did not ask it anything. It did not do anything. K.R.*

**The watch, the 13th.** On the River Flats' motion, seven hands flat: the roll entry above,
"for cause written. The cause is two entries in the Directions to the Unplaced, and what's in
the third column of each." Voted also that the bearer may lay the plate beside the School's
cast, in her own hand.

**The School's box.** A plaster cast in yellowed wool. Label under the lid: *Cast of the plate
of nine rings that was carried by the first teachers. Taken by leave of the bearer, for the
School's case, that students may know its look. The plate went out again with the Home Line
the same day, on its cord. It is not the School's and never was. We do not show the plate. We
show this.* The label has no date on the page. **The Reader:** the fifth ring is wider on both;
a flaw across the third ring at about four o'clock on both. "That is the plate of nine rings
that the first teachers carried. It was never preserved by anybody. It was *released.*" Noa had
drawn the rings from the fourth edition in the sixth month with the question *Is hers the
same?* The tenth edition will say so "out of the fourth. Word for word." Custody and history
only. The first book is still lost. What the plate is for: unknown; the Ferren leaves her
question in the book "with nothing under it."

**The keeper's book of the Ferren pump house, 2604** (found by Mara; read on the 6th): *The
11th of the third month. Keeper took ill in the evening; a fever, three nights. Entered here
because the needles did it too. / Dreamt that the roof was off the house and there was nothing
over it. Not dark. Nothing. And all four-and-twenty stood up together, and were looking. I do
not know at what. I woke and they were as usual. / The levee lamps went out that night and came
on again of themselves. No fault found. Not grid. I have put that in the fault column and this
in remarks, as I am told that a fever is not a fault.* Kiva sets it beside the one phrase she
is "allowed to have read," *the sky unroofed,* and "paying attention." **This is said under
the noise of three pumps, out of earshot:** Lumb has gone back to his sump, Aske and Kellan's
mother are at the door lamp putting the tin under wax, and Mr. Adlam stands at the far end of
the wall of needles, "out of hearing of the desk." None of the four hears that Kiva has
dreamed. Annick's page is given no date ("long before this. The page had no date"); Mara says
only that the two accounts are "lifetimes apart." Lumb comes back for his book and is told to
keep the page. Mara: "It doesn't tell
me what it is… I'd not build a shed on it… [It tells me] that it isn't you." Lumb: every pump
house on the coast keeps a remarks column.

**The Measure Hall board, the 14th.** *The Meridian Collegiate Circuit, 2680. The season is
read. / The Regional Measure: 1. Halden License. 2. Old Four. 3. Team Twenty-Eight. 4. T7.* (no
brackets). *The first company goes forward to the National Measure, at Saltmere, in the third
month, 2681. Entries under each team's own hand. / Entered by the Chair. Rowan, K., Third
Division, the Crooked Line: seated. No longer provisional. Catcher. Her sponsor's line and
every condition on her certificate stand as written. She is not thereby classified. I still
have no column. I have a page, and it has a second line on it. Y. Crest.* And, on Dray's
request, the mast condition "sent to Saltmere under seal," with both her statements entered.

**Rhea Sorn's card:** *Rowan, K. Catcher's second, to Pike. The night van, two nights in the
month, from the first of the new year, when her healer's card is up. Dark hands. No door on my
floor that is not on her certificate. Finch with her, for the cord, if Finch will. If T7 is
called on a night of mine, she is T7's and I go short. Until the last of the third month. Then
we look again, and either of us may say no. No seat is promised. R.S.* Kiva's two conditions:
the *before* column read out each night to all; her four have the first word. Signed on the
back by four on the 15th. It is to go back to Rhea on the first of the eleventh month.

**Jessamy Hart's card** (by the coast boat, the 14th): *Your letter was four lines. So is this.
/ I am a pump-fitter. Our harbour bank went into step on the 17th, at twenty past two. I knew
what it was before anybody told me. I knew it with the hand, the bad one. / Tell the one who
writes everything down that I should like to read it. / J.H.* He does not say that he forgives.
Kiva has not been shown it on the page; Orla puts it away when the juniors come.

## 5. The grid and the string (shown, not explained)

**What the page shows about the Step.**
1. The three Ferren pumps, on a bank in step, take their stroke together. Since the 17th of the
   ninth month the Ferren main, the storm drain for that side of the city, rises and falls on a
   count of four and four: two and a half fingers at the foot of Stopgate, a hair under the
   School's wall, and growing at the top of the hill.
2. The drums fell in in the order the main reaches them, two days apart: 19, 20, 21, then
   **25, (26, 27 dry), 28, 29.** Drums 22, 23 and 24 on the Kiln loop, where the copper leaves
   the drain, never fell in.
3. Two quenched drums did not stop it. Filled fresh, they were in step inside sixty counts.
4. In a lead-lined trough, a single plain cell on a stool, joined to a stepped bench bank by
   nothing but twelve paces of rising and falling water, leaned for an hour and went in.
5. **Nobody says that water can step a cell.** Mara: "the water in a box." Adlam's patent does
   not mention water. Why a tear's hold put the bank into step in the first place is not
   touched.

**The drill, the 2nd** (the Halden license yard; Birch's slate; a contained six-knot string):
1. The string ignored a lit gate twelve paces off, walked to the far end of the trough, and
   put two cords into the water. "It doesn't want the bright thing. It wants the thing that
   keeps time."
2. Kellan cut the top rung on Yarrow's word, with the cloth by the book hard against the
   trough. The open ash ran across the canvas and up the cord into the water. Yarrow bagged
   under thirty. **All six bench cells cooked together inside a breath;** the pump stopped.
3. **With the pump dead the water went on rising and falling. The string was pumping it.**
4. A fresh can on the stool went in at fifty-eight counts.
5. A boat-hook under the cord frosted from end to end and was taken; the fitter's left glove
   cracked.
6. Pru's ward, for one tap on the slack, folded before the haul, lifted the cord. Called half a
   count late by Kiva, it nipped Pru to the first joint. Called by Brask, it lifted clean, and
   **the water missed a beat and the cell on the stool fell out for a count.**
7. With the bung pulled and the trough empty, the cords hung ten counts and came apart into
   loose ash; the string read again and went to the gate.

**The string of the 5th.** Nobody on the page gives it a new name. The page calls it "a string
laid on its side" and "a string abreast"; Brask calls it a hold with nobody keeping it; Vane's
slate says "a string of twelve, abreast, every knot reading." It is never called a Barrel, and
no yield class is given.
1. The tear lies in the surface of the road over a shallow drain that is already keeping time,
   and runs on uphill toward ground that is not yet in step.
2. Knots rise through it side by side, eight paces apart, each on two legs, each reading,
   joined by a cord from which a frost hem hangs to the setts and stands above.
3. It does not turn to a lit gate. A head turns to a lit cell box in a house, and the cord
   bellies toward it.
4. Cut at the bottom end, the cord whips back to the next knot, and a new knot comes up at the
   top: "It's being paid out." Uncut, it pays out all the same at the drain's own pace until
   it runs out of road; a cut only lets the next knot out at once (the ninth and tenth rose on
   Rhea Sorn's two cuts; the eleventh and twelfth rose with nobody cutting). Twelve rose; ten
   stood when they were cut.
5. Where the drain dives, the tear stops. It then reaches with an arm to the nearest open
   water.
6. With its arm in the main it hauls and eases the main; the water goes on moving with the
   pumps stopped.
7. Taken off the water and planted in dry ground, with the sluice shut behind it and the bank
   dead, it had nothing to drive. Cut on one word with the corner given to the ground, the hem
   lay down. The tear, no longer held, gave three husks and one ordinary string and was closed
   by the Home Line in under an hour.
8. Its ash is loud. What came up after the hem was down "reads as this year's ash reads, and no
   louder."

**How the bank was taken out of step** (the method the page shows): nothing in the water that
keeps time; the pumps stopped; every cell quenched on one word; fresh cans, each salted with a
pinch of plain ash that "has never heard a beat"; a card under each handle with its own count,
"not two alike in this room"; a reader standing between each cell and the next for a slow ten;
a cell that falls back in is quenched and filled again; each pump restarted on its own eight
cells. Twenty-six minutes. It needs the main to be still, which on the night needed the arm
off the water and the sluice down.

**Instrument evidence.**
- **The notch and the lamps came apart.** The notch on Kiva's pen fell eleven seconds after her
  second cliff, as on both earlier occasions. The lamps (every window in the School, six
  hundred on the hill, and twenty-four dead needles at the pump house, which lifted "by a hair"
  with nothing in a single can) jumped about five minutes later, at *back,* when the hem came
  down. At the Pit the two fell in the same instant. Noa: "One of them keeps time with you. The
  other keeps time with that… I don't know what either is." Whether other masts showed the
  notch on the 5th is not on the page.
- **Noa's three columns.** People: median Hold 6.1 against forty years at 5.3 to 5.5; three
  Fire readings at seventeen; Stonehand's plate 340 for thirty-one years, then 350, 355, 362,
  380 since the thaw. The other thing: tears on the district board, fourteen against a usual
  four or five; skins, none in sixty years and nine this year; a Flask that walked a bus; a hold
  stood by husks; a string abreast. Both rise, and faster since the fifth month. Saltmere
  (Yeo's wire): a harbour bank of thirty-six cells in step from twenty past two on the 17th of
  the ninth month and not out; tears on the coast board nineteen against seven; skins eleven;
  the Saltmere school's plate beaten four times. **A line Noa will not vouch for:** a ship's
  engineer "off a hull from over the water" says that his own port's bank did the same at the
  same hour. "Not entered as fact."
- Nobody on the page says why. "It's two columns." Sent to the Charter Board under the Chair's
  seal, Adlam second, with a line of Yeo's and Lumb's notion of asking who else has a keeper
  with a book.

## 6. Ledgers

**Team Seven's book.**

| Date | Entry | Sealed medical (W) | Held | Notes |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| Sun 25th, 9th (carried) | Held 5.62; 18.0 credited from the Pit | 2.0 | 5.62 | The 18.0 is entered in the next line. |
| **Wed 28th, 9th** | **Signed by four** | 2.0 | 14.12 | In hand 5.62 + 18.0 = 23.62. Call reserve, half a dram a fighter, five (Pryce, Cade, Renn, Rowan, Talley by name): 2.5. Second purse sheet and poles, "Finch to catch when Rowan is otherwise seated": 3.0. Lent at par to the casualty reserve for the boxes of the streets on dry drums, to be repaid: 4.0 (Perrin's figure). |
| Tue 4th, 10th | Loan repaid | 2.0 | 14.12 | Stated in the closing entry; entered with it. |
| **Wed 5th** | **Signed by four** | 2.0 | 13.53 | *Three-door, K. Rowan: 0.59, from held. One go. Only on a foreman's direction, under the officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer. Cade keeps the word. If not directed, returned to held, whole.* |
| **Thu 6th, 12:40 a.m.** | **Seal broken by four** | **0.8** | 13.53 | *For: the reading hand of M. Rowan, operator, Grade Two, the Municipal Works, starved in taking the Ferren bank out of step. On the word of a grader and by the hand of Healer Aske. Medical. Drawn: 1.2. Re-sealed: 0.8.* |
| **Sat 15th** | **Signed by four** | 0.8 | **25.53** | Call reserve spent 0.70 (Pryce 0.41, Rowan none; the repaired page reports the old lines in narration and no longer gives Cade 0.14, Talley 0.09 and Renn 0.06, which stand as in the frozen first draft); returned 1.80. Three-door line spent whole. Loan of 4.0 repaid. *Credited from Stopgate, by the foreman's pot: 9.0. To Talley, J., Kill, by name, a head's share: 1.8. To the book: 7.2.* Held 26.53. Then, on Jude's proposal: *Returned at par to the wardens of Stopgate, for the boxes on drum twenty-nine: 1.0.* **Held 25.53.** |

Check: 23.62 − 3.0 (sheet) − 0.70 (call drams spent) − 0.59 (three-door) + 7.2 (pot) = 26.53;
less 1.0 returned = **25.53.** The loan of 4.0 went out and came back. Sealed medical: 2.0 −
1.2 = **0.8,** and it cannot be made up: there is no plain ward-trace to be bought. Jude Talley
has 6.0 + 1.8 = **7.8** by name. Kiva's two step tests on the night came off her own weekly
half dram and are not on the team's book. Pru is paid off the Circuit's reserve card. No
warrant for Saltmere has been posted or entered; Kellan reckons ten for five heads.

**Refused on the page (the 4th):** the sealed reserve as salt for the bank ("It's medical");
the red-tagged bags as bait ("I wrote *Not for the ground*… I can't weigh what it would do";
Halloran: "Nobody burns the only one of anything while I've the key"). The red bags of the
ninth month are not touched in this movement, and the claim before the Compact's court is not
mentioned.

**The reference shelf.** Twenty-four jars of plain Handful ash written off by number, with the
reason, Mara signing under Halloran (one read out: *Sag Line. The 4th of the third month, 2511.
Plain. Held 106*). A further thirty for the drums refused: "A shelf that gives whenever it's
asked nicely isn't a shelf." The seven drums still in step are to be done with the wardens'
old winter stock.

**Vane's slate, read on the 6th at five:** *Stopgate, the night of the 5th. The bell at two
minutes past eleven. A tear lying in the road, held by the main under it. A string of twelve,
abreast, every knot reading. Closed, ten minutes to one, by the Home Line. / Lives: none lost.
Carried: sixty-two houses, fourteen by litter, and a school; the last of the top stretch down
by the curb, behind a walking wall, when the gate was taken. Hurt: K. Rowan, catcher, the left
arm; see the direction. M. Rowan, operator, the reading hand, starved; eased inside the hour.
Cade, carry, two ribs sprung and not broken. Works: the School's core put down by its keeper
and raised again at noon. The Stopgate sluice shut, the first time in the Works' memory, and
opened at first light. The Ferren bank quenched and filled on four-and-twenty counts; it is
out. The pumps stood six-and-twenty minutes. Eight cellars in the Hythe, a hand of water. /
Drum twenty-nine, fouled. A plank split with the frost under the first knot, and the best part
of thirty drams went down. Its four streets are on boxes; the reserve says a month. Seven drums
below it are in step still, each alone on an open link. The Works will take them out one in a
day. / Tinned, clean: a hundred and ninety-two… Put by, red, on catchers' words and a comb: five
hundred and sixty and four-tenths. And a stripe of something that isn't in the yield book. /
Remarks: one. The one hole on the hill that could not be planked was left open, with a boy in
it, to clear a gate. The thing went to it. The reason for leaving it was good, and I'd give the
same order tomorrow. The remark is the event's.*

**The pot,** the Line's way, "in the Pit's own parts," ground first: the civic half (96 of the
192) into the hill's boxes and the Hythe's the same night; then the casualty reserve (24) and
the schools (36); the teams last (36), **9 to each of the four fours
that stood it under their officers** (Halden License, Old Four, Twenty-Eight, Seven). After the
repair the page gives the half and the nine; the 24 and the two 36s follow from the Pit's parts
and are no longer spoken. The red
row is twelve bags, each under a catcher's name. Where the red row goes is not said on the
page.

**Not on any slate:** a can of civic stock in the Works' own store was cut with soot and would
not hold its tune at the eleventh cell. It is noticed and replaced; nobody takes it up.

## 7. New people and places

People (pronouns as written; author's choice):
- **Ansty** (she; keeper of the School's core house; short; a ring of keys; a red lever with a
  pin).
- **Lumb** (he; old; keeper of the Ferren pump house; a watch and a notched stick).
- **Ned** (he; fourteen; a Works fitter's boy; the rake; then the runner at the lodge door).
- **Officer Yeo** (she; a Saltmere company officer in green; brought the Saltmere house four to
  the Regional; cut the fourth knot; went south on the 8th).
- **Mr. Garland** of number fifty-eight, and his bird, **Admiral.**
- **Bettony** (she; a tall junior). The sandy junior with the plait is not named; she is about
  thirteen and was Kindled in the spring.
- Kellan's mother signs **"A. Renn, grader."** She is otherwise still unnamed.
- Unnamed: the School's bursar (he); the fitter in the leather apron who came with a saw; the
  Works apprentice on the bicycle; the warden of drum house twenty-five and the eleven wardens
  of Stopgate; a School porter; the Works clerk at the sounder; six of the ten blades and most
  of the catchers on the hill (the slate says only that the Halden four cut two knots and
  caught one); Twenty-Eight's ward and reserve blade,
  still.
- Returning by name: Hendry Moss of Stonehand (chapter 15), Bettan, Nairn, Tibb, Stiles, Doran
  Mace, Ruddock, Greta Stroud, Halloran, the Reader, Mrs. Pryce (seen, floury, not speaking).

Places and things:
- **Stopgate:** the long straight hill from the Brickfields road to the School's bottom wall, a
  tram line up the middle, sixty-two houses on the hill itself. (The six hundred households
  put on boxes were the streets fed by the two dry drums, most of them to either side of the
  hill, with roads of their own.) Drum houses twenty-five (the foot) to twenty-nine (near the
  top; the upper end of the seam is "not forty paces below" the wicket, and by the knot
  spacing about thirty-two); a grating between the rails every twenty paces. Nobody had
  asked why it was called that.
- **The Kiln loop:** where the copper leaves the road after drum twenty-one and goes east round
  the old brick kilns (drums 22–24).
- **A drum house:** a brick hut with a green door, one drum, a needle under glass.
- **The School's bottom:** the bottom wall (thirty feet of brick) and the black oak cart gate
  with a wicket and the School's device, "a line with a kink in it"; the bottom yard, thirty
  paces square; the core house on the east with the Low Lane against its south wall and the
  New Lane behind; a low wall with a gap on the west; the Old Yard beyond the gap, forty paces
  to the sheds, two rows of posts, **the leaning post eight paces in, a yard and a half from
  the wall,** leaning out over the lane (to the left as one sees it from the sheds). It is the
  last of the south row as the lane is counted from the sheds and the first met from the gap.
  The eighth post is the last of the north row.
- **The sluice:** an iron lid where Kiva's cross was, on the lane side of the post's foot; a
  square brick shaft eight feet deep; an iron penstock in two grooves a foot above the water,
  "across the wall side of the shaft, back under the post"; a screw on the rake coming up
  inside the post from a gate "eight feet under and a pace back toward the wall"; a square socket in the "split"; an ash bar, now on two hooks on the wall. A second,
  small mouth from the core house enters above the gate. Nine turns shuts the hill from the low
  streets; shut, the hill's rain has nowhere to go.
- **The Ferren main:** the storm drain; shallow under Stopgate, eight feet down inside the
  gate; it comes in under the gate, runs west along the wall to the post, and goes on west
  under the south row.
- The Ferren pump house's sump hatch and sounder; the keeper's books in calf; the reference
  shelf's plain row; **the municipal wire** with a key in the keeper's lodge; the Charter
  Board; **the National Measure, at Saltmere.**

Names checked by search against every book's manuscript and registry in this repository and
`universe/` before use: Stopgate, Lumb, Ansty, Yeo, Garland, Admiral, Bettony, Ned, the Kiln
loop. One first choice was dropped: "Tam" (too near Tamar Vey of the universe bible) became
Ned. "Ostrey" was considered for a third Graywater blade and not used, since chapter 30 has it
as a name out of the past.

## 8. Plants and open threads

| Plant | Status at exit |
|---|---|
| Three doors open; no column | **Seated, unclassified.** A second line on the Chair's *Witnessed* page. No rank, Hold, Draw or bench figure is re-measured. |
| The crooked post | **Paid.** It is the handle of a sluice gate, "aimed," on a lane laid over a drain that turns. The manual kept the why; the Line kept the what. |
| A meter and a shutdown | The meter was a cork. The shutdown was under Kiva's cross. |
| The disk | Recognized as the plate of nine rings of the first teachers, released by appointment. Cold. **What it is for: unknown. The first book: lost.** |
| The dream | A second, independent line (a pump keeper, 2604) has the roof off, nothing over it, and every needle "looking," on a night in the fault column. No cause, date for Annick, meaning or use. **Not named on the page as the Unroofed Sky.** |
| The notch and T-0 | **Two things.** One follows Kiva's cliff by eleven seconds. One follows the fall of a hold. Neither is explained. |
| Human and spawn escalation | Two columns, and a third from Saltmere. Correlation and a quickening since the fifth month. No cause. |
| Two-finger sensation | As before. Permanent. |
| The Ferren bank | **Out of step.** |
| The Brickfields trunk | Seven drums (19, 20, 21, 25, 26, 27, 28) still in step, each alone on an open link; one a day. **Drum twenty-nine fouled;** four streets on boxes about a month. The School's core house never fell in. |
| Saltmere | A harbour bank of thirty-six in step since the same minute and not out. "Nobody at Saltmere… knows how to take a bank out." Yeo will ask for the Works' reader again in the spring. |
| The Charter Board | Three sheets sent. No answer on the page. |
| The National Measure | Saltmere, the third month, 2681. The first company goes: Halden License, Old Four, Twenty-Eight, Seven. Dray's mast rule goes with it. |
| Rhea Sorn | A bounded card, to the last of the third month. |
| Jessamy Hart | Answered. Wants to read what Kiva has written down. Kiva does not yet know. |
| Selka Dray | Unpersuaded; means to beat Kiva at Saltmere. |
| Team Seven | Fourth; second step in its own right; held 25.53; sealed 0.8. |
| Held tears | The Home Line's suspension is not mentioned again. |
| The red bags | 111 + 503.7 from before, untouched; 560.4 more from Stopgate. The court claim is not mentioned. |
| The Order of the Even Hand | **Not on the page in this movement.** Its door is still "on the latch." |
| Mara's sealed page; the 2446 entry; the first bearer's book | Untouched. |
| A cut can in the Works' store | Noticed. Open. |

## 9. Items for owner or editorial attention

Routes the author chose where the packet left room, and places where the draft may have gone
further than intended:

1. **The mechanism is a storm drain.** The Step walks the Ferren main because three stepped
   pumps make the water rise and fall. The page shows order, a sealed prediction, a field
   confirmation and a bench confirmation; it never says that water can step a cell. This turns
   Movement Five's "nine drums" into five and its eighteen days into ten (from the 27th).
2. **The first isolation plan is a firebreak,** and it fails without making anything worse. Its
   cost is six hundred households on boxes for five days and an examiner's public admission.
3. **The bad assumption in the drill** is "stop the pumps and the main lies still." A string
   with an arm in the water is itself a pump.
4. **The new expression is not named and not classed.** "A string laid on its side"; twelve
   rose and ten stood; 720 drams by the book of a knot, a Flask and a half. It is never called
   a Barrel, and the registry's "Breach" is not used. If the owner wants a name or a yield
   class for it, the foreman's slate or Halloran's shelf is the place.
5. **The crooked post is a working shut-off.** This is the largest invention of the movement.
   It rests on the prologue's plural ("the lane with the crooked posts") and on S.V.'s line,
   which stands unaltered. New on the page: the School straightened the lane in 2390; the
   Line's sheet; the penstock; "Stopgate." Review should check chapters 3, 8, 17 and 36 for
   anything that makes a spindle inside the post impossible (it is "black, split, two iron
   bands, leans left," and Kiva has laid a hand on it).
6. **Kiva's cross was over the sluice lid.** Eight feet of ground and a brick barrel are the
   page's reason that six clean stances never rang. Review should weigh it.
7. **What the three doors did** is again a catch, turned: the throw taken across the haul, the
   corner planted in ground chosen by a step test, **given to three still-doors at about nine,**
   and released minutes later on Kiva's word. She holds it for about four counts.
8. **The cost was the arm for four days, returned.** The packet forbade restoring the fingers
   and required the hand to close "as before." The page states the risk first and has Aske
   refuse to say why it came back.
9. **The sealed reserve was opened for Mara's reading hand,** on Anwen's proposal, with Kiva
   signing fourth. Mara's injury is new and heals; it costs her a month of bank work.
10. **The grid answer is local and provisional:** one bank out, seven drums to do, one drum
    fouled, Saltmere's bank still in.
11. **Kiva's public place is double:** the Home Line's roll (the Tansy Wold line of 2471,
    repeated) and the Chair's "seated. No longer provisional." The brackets come off Seven and
    Old Four. No one on the page says that the call counted as the owed Flask except Pike.
12. **Rhea Sorn's offer** is two nights a month as Pike's second, for three months. Kiva
    conditions it and takes it to her four, who sign.
13. **The disk's recognition** runs through a plaster cast and a label, with the Reader as the
    credible witness and Noa's drawing as the earlier notice. The label is undated. It asserts
    "the first teachers" and nothing earlier.
14. **The historical echo is a pump keeper of 2604,** not a Glory master. The universe bible
    says that "a small number of high-rank practitioners" record the fragments. The owner may
    prefer a higher-rank witness; the keeper was chosen because the 2604 T-0 entry already
    stood in the Works' register and because it makes the phenomenon wider as well as older.
15. **Saltmere's bank fell in the same minute,** and an unvouched line carries it overseas.
    This is the plant for Books Two and Three and should be checked against the series map.
16. **Jessamy Hart answers.** Four lines; a pump-fitter; "the hand, the bad one."
17. **Kellan takes the Halden yard** and stays on Seven's book. **Perrin's 380** is a new
    figure. **Rhea's sixth column** and **Selka Dray as count-caller** are the author's.
18. **Kellan's mother is given an initial** ("A. Renn"). **"A. Renn," Ansty, Lumb, Yeo, Ned,
    Garland and Bettony** are pending owner review, as are the pronouns in section 7.
19. **Movement length is 51,677 words after the repair and the post-recheck corrections**
    (`wc -w`; 50,866 in the first draft; 51,670 after the repair alone; 51,684 after the
    whole-book reconciliation, which added seven words to chapter 48). Chapters run 5,297 to 7,264. Forty-four sections, about 1,174 words a section. The
    viewpoint figures that follow are the first draft's and were not recounted. **Secondary viewpoint is 12.8%** by plain section
    word count (Mara 1,730; Anwen 1,294; Rhea 1,131; Kellan 1,035; Perrin 863; Orla 439; all
    established lenses); Kiva holds 87.2%. These are counts, not craft measurements. The
    formula's targets were written toward and have not been measured.
20. Small facts taken from the checkpoints and not re-read in the manuscript should be checked:
    the layout of the Old Yard and the bottom yard against chapters 3, 9 and 19; that the
    School's core house has an iron door and no window; that Rhea Sorn was pulled from a culvert
    in the spring of 2657, at nineteen (checked in the whole-book reconciliation; the first
    draft's "autumn of '64" and "sixteen years" were wrong against chapters 8, 23 and 31); Stonehand's plate figures; Hendry Moss; the weir meadow's iron plate; the Reader's
    thirty-one years; that the fourth edition of the *Founders' History* has a plate of the
    rings; the district table's tear count of fourteen. Two
    small ambiguities are on the page: Vane says on the 10th that he "moved a thing" when the
    watch sat on the 9th, and on the 13th the River Flats makes the roll motion and the seven
    vote in Kiva's presence; and that motion cites "two entries" in the Directions to the
    Unplaced, where Kiva has three directions (the two with a cost column are meant).
21. Arithmetic worth a mechanical check: the drain dates (25 on the 27th; 28 on the 3rd; 29 on
    the 5th; the core house due the 7th); "ten" days; the knot numbering (twelve named, the eighth
    and the seventh cut early, ten standing; positions at eight paces from 29's rail to the gate);
    the red figure 560.4; the pot (96, 24, 36, 36, 9); the team book; "eleven weeks and four
    days" to the new year; seventy-seven years since 2603; the pumps' twenty-six minutes
    against Lumb's twenty-five.
22. **Chapters were committed by another process as each landed.** Small continuity corrections
    and two additions were made in the working tree afterward (see `AUTHORSHIP.md`). The
    working tree is the first draft.

## 10. Deliberately not done

The disk does not warm, teach, speak, map, choose, open a door or answer; it is recognized and
it is cold. The dream does not recur; the name Unroofed Sky is not on the page; nobody
identifies the figures, the filter, the Makers, the cause of the Fall or the disk's origin. The
keeper's line and the Annick phrase are set side by side as testimony and nobody builds on
them. Nobody says why a drain steps a cell, why a tear throws stepped ash, why husks stood a
hold, or what the notch or the lamps are. The three-door configuration is done once, under a
written direction and an officer's hand, offered and not asked, with the price said first by
her healer; it is not certified, ranked, classified, taught or repeated, and no rank or bench
figure is re-measured. The fingers are not cured by ash, rank, rest or the result. The sealed
reserve is opened only as medicine and entered as such; the red bags are not used; no free
practice ash appears. Students close no tear, cut no knot except under a foreman's slate, and
touch no municipal cell except the Bench-passed students at the pump house under the Works'
operators. The Home Line closes the tear. The Municipal Works and the Halden Works stay
distinct, and neither is a villain; nor is the School. Selka Dray is not made a friend. No
secondary viewpoint is new. The offer is bounded and conditioned. Book Two is not started; its
invitations are a notice, a card, a wire and three sheets. No commit, push, publication,
narration, review, scoring or repair was done by the author.

## 11. What Book Two opens on

State on the evening of Sunday the 16th of the tenth month, 2680:

- **The grid.** Meridian has taken one municipal bank out of step by a method that is written
  down in seven columns and on its way to the coast. Seven drums wait their turn. Saltmere's
  harbour bank is still one engine. A ship's engineer says that it is not only this country.
- **The rifts.** A tear can lie down in a road over water that keeps time, pay out a string
  abreast, and pump. A long-forgotten sluice stopped one. Nobody knows how many
  drains have a gate.
- **The record.** Three sheets are before the Charter Board. A pump keeper of 2604 and an Order's
  Master, on a page with no date, have both written that there was no roof. Every pump house on the coast has a
  remarks column that nobody has read. A plate has been recognized and has said nothing.
- **The people.** Kiva is seated, unclassified, on the Line's roll, stood down from every door
  until the new year, with a company's card to the end of the third month and eight fingers
  that report. Seven is fourth, in its own right, with 25.53 held and 0.8 sealed, and goes to
  the National Measure at Saltmere in the third month with Halden License, Old Four and
  Twenty-Eight. Anwen has a summer on the night van. Perrin has his Hall's second team. Kellan
  has a yard. Jude has a yield line. Pru has a sheet and a red tag. Selka Dray will be on her
  feet. Mara has one reading hand on a card until the eleventh month. Orla has a card from the
  south coast.
- Withheld for Book Two and after, intact: what the disk is for; the first book; the cause of
  the Step; why a hold stood; what the notch and the lamps are; who else has dreamed; the
  filter; the Makers; Kiva's classification; whether three doors can be made safe, taught, or
  done twice.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-41.md

# Chapter 41 — Kiva: Not the Copper

There was a boy on the Coper Street step when the tram let them down, and he had a slate-blue card in his fist.

He was one of Graywater's runners. He had plainly been told to wait for a girl with a strapped knee, and he looked at Kiva's knee before he looked at her face.

"From the Torch," he said. "I'm to see you read it."

It was three words long. *Yard. Now. Van.*

The bells were still going. There were three of them, and they did not keep together. Mast five rang its two and two from over the Sag. The weigh-house rang three and three. The third came up off the river on a code that Kiva did not know, and each of them was a little out with the other two, so that the whole sky over the east bank sounded like a clockmaker's shop at noon.

"That's an attached team turned out," said Mara. She had the trunk book under her arm still. "Go on. I'll not be at home in any case. Rusk will want a reader at mast five within the hour, and I'm the only one he has who'll go up a mast at dusk without being paid double."

"You said it wasn't mine."

"It isn't. A bell is. You're on a company's card." Mara looked at her hand, and then did not say what she had been going to. "Have you your book?"

"In my coat."

"Then put what you see on the left and what you think on the right, and don't mix them. I shall be doing the same thing a mile off, and I'd like one of us to be tidy."

Kiva went with the boy.

---

The Graywater watch room had three lamps lit on its map wall, and nobody in it was pretending to be calm.

They were calm all the same. That was the difference between a company and a school. Rhea Sorn stood in front of the wall with her coat on and her hands in its pockets, and read the three small lights as a carter reads a street.

"The weir. The weigh-house. Mast five." She did not turn round. "Three bells inside a minute, on three masts that are a mile apart. I've been twenty-three years in this room and I've seen two together, once, in a flood. Imrie, you've the second van and the weigh-house; it's a street, so it's yours. The Home Line has mast five. I'll take the weir." The pale eyes came round at last and went along the wall bench. "Seven."

They were all there. Anwen had come from the laundry with flour on her cuff. Perrin had come at a run and was pretending that he had not. Kellan stood at the end in his own coat, and Jude Talley beside him, and Pru had her yellow card out already, held in both hands.

"You're attached. You'll ride with me, and you'll do what you're entered for and nothing else. Rowan."

"Recovery," said Kiva. "Dark hands. And I'm barred any go until the first; Aske has it on a card." She held up the left. "Two don't report. Finch has the cord from the first turn. That's arranged."

"I know that it is. I was in the Pit." Rhea took her hands out of her pockets. "Say it anyway. Say it every time. Pike."

"Two sheets on the van," said the square catcher. "And I've a word to say to that first-year about her third turn, on the way."

It was dusk when they came down the bank into the weir meadow.

Kiva had sat on that bank in the spring with sixty other candidates and watched Graywater take a Flask to pieces. It looked smaller without one. There was the long grass, grey in the last of the light, and the weir beyond it making its one continual noise, and the mast on its three legs with the bell under it still swinging.

There was nothing else.

Four wards in blue brought a gate up at the head of the meadow all the same. It stood there, silver and patient, lighting the seed-heads. Rhea walked the ground behind it with her hands in her pockets. She walked the whole of it, and she looked behind her twice.

"What's there?" she said.

"Nothing," said Anwen. "The grass isn't lying. There's no cold. I've stood at the tape of a tear all day, and the back of your neck knows before the rest of you." She frowned. "Mine doesn't know anything."

"Cade."

"A weir. A mast. A bell that somebody ought to stop." Perrin considered. "And a cow, on the far bank, who's as surprised as I am."

"Renn."

"There's no light in it," said Kellan slowly. "A tear's blue from the side. I've walked round that mast and it's only an evening."

Rhea did not ask Kiva. She looked at her, which was a different thing.

Kiva had been standing on something. She had known it through her boot for a minute or more, without attending to it, as one knows that a tram is coming before one hears it. She knelt down in the grass.

It was a square of iron, a yard across, sunk level with the turf. She had written a line about that plate in the spring, with a question mark after it. *Stopped on an iron plate in the grass, 2–3 counts, on its way to the wall.*

She laid her right palm on it, and beside that the heel of her left.

It came up slow and even and many. It was not strong. It was the hum of the tram sheds heard from the far end of a street. It was the thing that she had felt in a doorpost on the Brickfields road at four o'clock that afternoon, with her aunt beside her counting needles.

"It's keeping time," she said. "This is. There's water under it."

"That's the sluice feeder," said Pike. He had come up behind her with a roll of cord. "It runs under the meadow to the Ferren main."

"Then the main's keeping time." She heard how that sounded, and took her hands off the iron. "I can't weigh it. It's what comes through a plate. I'd not have anybody stand a wall down on my saying so."

"Nobody's standing one down. You were asked what's there." Rhea had her slate out. She wrote on it, briefly, and it was not in the fourth column. "You've an aunt reading drums for the Municipal Works. Tell her. Tonight, in those words, with the *can't weigh it* left in."

A man was coming down the bank with a lantern. He came at a walk, as he came everywhere, and he had his hat on.

"Nothing at five," said Hollis Vane. "I've had a closing crew stand round an empty field for an hour like mourners at the wrong grave. Imrie's sent from the weigh-house. Nothing there but a carter who thinks that we owe him a night's sleep." He stopped beside the gate and looked at four wards burning ash for the benefit of a meadow. "Stand it down, Sorn, if you're content. I've the reason, such as it is. It came from her aunt, up a ladder."

"Go on."

"A mast rings when its drum climbs past a mark. The mark's set on the grid. Mrs. Rowan says that the three drums didn't climb. She says the mark came down to meet them, for about as long as it takes to breathe in, and went back." He said it flatly, like a casualty list. "All three inside the same minute. She says it'll do so again at the top of some other breath, tonight or Thursday, and she can't tell me which. She says she can stop the bells by having the marks moved."

"And then they'll not ring when there is one," said Rhea.

"She said that as well. She said it first."

Nobody spoke. The four wards let their wall go out, one after another, and the meadow went dark as a room does when a lamp is carried from it.

"Then a bell's no use to me until further word," said Rhea. "I'll have eyes on every mast in my district by morning. People, sitting, with a drum in front of them and a boy to run." She put the slate away. "That's forty hands I haven't got."

"You've a Circuit full of students who can read a tape," said Kellan. He said it to the grass. "There's one of them reads it better than anybody in Meridian. I'd begin with him."

"Bexley."

"He'd do it for nothing. He'd very likely pay you."

Rhea almost smiled. It did not reach her mouth.

"Write me his name on the back of that card, Renn, and the names of nine more who won't fall asleep." She turned to go up the bank. "Finch. Pike has a word to say to you about a cord."

He said it in the van, by the light of the shuttered lamp, the whole way back along the Brickfields road. Pru crossed a waxed cord on her knee four-and-thirty times with the square grey catcher watching her hands, and got it right on thirty.

"Four," said Pike. "That's four that'd be a lost bag. Do you know which four?"

"The ones where I was thinking about it."

"That's the first sensible thing anybody's said tonight." He took the cord from her and laid it across her palm again. "Then you'll do it until you're not."

---

Mara went to drum twenty-two on Tuesday evening, since it was twenty-two's turn, and she took Kiva to carry the books.

"The records rate doesn't run to a clerk," she said. "You'll stand on the pavement. You'll hold the lamp to the page when I ask. You're not to come over a sill, and if you feel anything through a wall you're to say so in the same words that you used to Sorn."

"I told you those."

"You did. I've had them in my head all day, like a tune."

A drum house was not much to look at, and Kiva had walked past a hundred of them without seeing one. It was a brick hut no bigger than a coal store, set back from the road behind an iron rail, with a slate roof and a green door and a number on the lintel. Inside was one drum, taller than a man, that fed three or four streets. There was a needle under glass on its face.

Twenty-two stood on the Kiln loop. The copper left the Brickfields road at the gasworks corner, after the twenty-first drum, and went east in a long bend round the old brick kilns and the rows of small houses that had been built for the kiln hands. It came back to the road a mile farther on. Mara had explained that on the tram, with her finger on the plan.

"There," she said, from inside the door. "Come to the sill and look. That's a drum minding its own business."

The needle stood. It trembled a little, as a needle does, on a time of its own.

"It's due," said Kiva.

"It's due tonight, if the thing keeps the time that it's kept. Nineteen on the Wednesday, twenty on the Friday, twenty-one on Sunday." Mara had her left palm on the drum's iron side. "I can't feel that it's so much as thinking of it."

They waited. It got dark. A lamplighter went along the row on his lift-board, a hand's height off the paving, touching each lamp awake. Mara read the needle every quarter of an hour and wrote the figure, and Kiva held the lamp, and nothing whatever happened.

At twenty minutes to nine somebody came round the bend of the loop on a Works bicycle, very fast, ringing the bell.

"*Mrs. Rowan!*" It was a boy in an apprentice's apron. He had no breath. "Mr. Rusk says—you're to come—it's gone in. Not here. It's twenty-five."

"Twenty-five," said Mara.

"On Stopgate. The foot of the hill. The warden there had her hand on the door for her eight o'clock and saw it nodding." He leaned on the handlebars. "It's gone past three. It's skipped the whole loop. Mr. Rusk says it's got faster. He says, if it can do three in a night—"

"I can do that sum for myself, thank you." Mara had already shut the green door and turned the key. "Give me the plan, Kiva. No. Hold it. Walk."

It was twelve minutes to the foot of Stopgate, going quickly.

Kiva knew the street, as everybody at the Crooked Line knew it. It was the long hill that climbed from the Brickfields road to the bottom wall of the school, straight as a rule, with a tram line up the middle and tall narrow houses on both sides. She had gone up it every morning for six months. She had never once wondered why it was called that.

There was a small crowd at the rail of the twenty-fifth drum house. A woman with a warden's handbell stood at the open door with her arms folded.

Mara went in. Kiva stopped at the rail.

She could see the needle from there. It went up, and it came down. It did it slowly, and with a horrible ease, like something that had been doing it for years.

"When?" said Mara's voice.

"I can't say when. I looked at eight. It was nodding at eight."

"And at seven?"

"I don't look at seven."

Kiva was not listening any longer. She had turned round.

It was behind her. She had felt it as she came along the pavement, in the soles of her feet, and had taken it for the tram. There was no tram. The line up the middle of Stopgate lay empty under the lamps, wet and shining, and the hum was not in the rails.

It was in the crown of the road. She walked out to it.

There was a grating there, between the rails, a yard long, of the old thick iron. She had stepped over it every morning of the summer. She knelt beside it, on the wet setts, and put her right palm flat on the bars.

Slow, and even, and many.

And there was a sound under it. It was a long way down. It was water, and it was not running as water runs in a drain. It drew in, with a soft hollow noise, like breath taken through the teeth. Then it let go.

In. And out.

"Mara," she said. She did not shout it. "It's here. It isn't at the wall. It's in the road."

Her aunt came out to the rail.

"Say it properly."

"There's a grating. There's water under it, and the water's keeping time. I can hear it. Four in, and four out." She took her hand away. "I can't weigh it. I can't. But you could look. It's only a hole with water in it."

Mara stood quite still for a moment. Then she took the lamp out of Kiva's hand, and came through the rail, and knelt down on the setts in her work skirt, and lowered the lamp on its chain between the bars as far as the chain would go.

It lit a round brick throat, green with slime, and at the bottom of it a black shine.

The shine rose. It came up the brick by the width of two fingers, and left a wet mark, and went down again.

Mara watched it do that six times. Her lips moved.

"That's the Ferren main," she said. She said it very quietly, to the hole. "It's the storm drain for the whole of this side. It runs from the top of this hill to the sump under the pump house, and three pumps lift what's in it over the levee." She did not look up. "Three pumps, on a bank of four-and-twenty. I've stood in that house for nine days and listened to them all take their stroke together. I thought that it was rather restful."

"It isn't in the copper," said Kiva.

"No. I said so yesterday, and I thought I was saying that I didn't know where it was." Mara drew the lamp up, hand over hand. "Give me the plan. Hold the corner down; it's wet."

She spread it on the setts, under the lamp, and Kiva held it with the heel of her left hand.

"There's the copper. Twenty-one at the gasworks. Then it goes off round the kilns, twenty-two, twenty-three, twenty-four, and comes back in here, at the foot of the hill. Twenty-five." Her finger went back to the gasworks corner. It did not follow the red line. It went straight up the road, along a thin blue one that Kiva had taken for the edge of the paper. "And there's the main. It doesn't go round the kilns. Why should it? Water won't climb a bend to oblige a brickmaker. It comes straight down Stopgate and straight along the road."

"So twenty-five's the next one. On the drain."

"On the drain, twenty-five is the next after twenty-one. It hasn't skipped anything. It's kept its two days to the hour." Mara sat back on her heels. "And the three on the loop aren't due at all. They never were."

The warden had come to the rail to see what the Works was doing on its knees in the road. The apprentice had come with her.

"Then it hasn't got faster," said the boy.

"It hasn't got faster. It's got nearer." Mara's finger moved up the blue line. There were five numbers on it between the foot of the hill and the top. "Six-and-twenty. Seven. Eight. Nine. And the core house. I said eighteen days yesterday, to my niece, in a doorway." She took her hand off the paper. "It's ten. If it keeps its time."

Nobody said anything. In the hole between them the water drew in, and let go.

"Is that a reason?" said Kiva.

"It's one drum." Mara began to fold the plan. Her voice was level, and Kiva knew that levelness; it was the voice in which she read a figure that she did not like. "The first three were on the copper and the drain alike, so they tell me nothing. I've a single drum out of its turn, and a drain underneath it, and a girl who says that she can't weigh what she felt. That's a result. A man could drive a cart through what it isn't."

"What would make it a reason?"

"A second one. Called beforehand, in writing, by somebody who stood to look a fool." She got up, and brushed the wet from her knees, and it did no good. "I shall have to go and be that person in front of Rusk. I'd hoped for a quiet autumn."

---

They had the Chair's hearing room again on Wednesday morning, and this time there were more than forty.

The Municipal Works had the left of the long table. Examiner Rusk sat in the middle of it, broad and tidy, with linen guards over his cuffs and the trunk plan pinned out before him under four brass weights. He had three of his own people. He had also Mr. Adlam, who sat a little apart from them in his black coat, as a guest sits who has been asked for one thing only.

The wardens of Stopgate had the right. There were eleven of them. The School had sent its bursar, a thin worried man in a gown, and the keeper of its core house, who was a short woman named Ansty with a ring of keys at her belt and did not look worried at all.

Rhea Sorn and Hollis Vane sat together at the far end, which Kiva had not seen before.

She was not at the table. She was on the wall bench, behind Mara, with the books on her knee, there to hand up a volume when it was asked for, and she had been told so.

"I'll say what I am not," said Ysra Crest. "I am not the Works, and I'm not the Line. I've a Circuit, and a school of that Circuit stands at the head of a hill with seven hundred students in it. Mr. Rusk has been good enough to ask for this room. I've lent it. It's his."

Rusk stood up.

He did it heavily, with his scarred thumbs on the edge of the table.

"Then I'll be short, since I've been wrong once this week already. On Monday I had a thing walking my trunk at a drum in two days, and nine drums to spare. Last night it was at the foot of Stopgate with five." He looked along the wardens. "There are two accounts of how it came there. I'll give you mine, and then I'll have my reader give you hers, since she'll not thank me for softening it."

"Yours first," said Ysra.

"It goes from ash to ash. That's the only way that anybody has ever seen a cell take a beat, in three hundred years of the trade. Mr. Adlam will bear me out; it's his house's patent." Adlam inclined his head. "A bank's put into step by salting it with ash that already keeps one. So I say that something is carrying from drum to drum, along my trunk, by a road I can't see, and that last night it found a short way. If I'm right, it wants ash to walk on. I can take the ash away."

He put a thumb on the plan.

"Six-and-twenty, and seven-and-twenty. I quench the pair of them tonight and I leave them dry for a week. Two dead drums, halfway up the hill. A firebreak. Whatever it is can come up to the edge of that and look across."

"And the streets on those two?" said a warden.

"On their boxes. Six hundred households, for a week, in the tenth month. A box is three days. So I'll want cans, and the casualty reserve has undertaken to find them." Rusk glanced up the table. "It'll be asking the Circuit's teams to lend at par, I'm told. I'd not have chosen to come to students with my cap out."

"We'll speak of that," said Ysra. "Mrs. Rowan."

Mara did not stand. She laid her left hand flat on the table, as she laid it on a drum.

"It didn't find a short way. It went to the next drum that the Ferren main runs under. I've the plan. I've the hour for each of the four. And the water in that main is rising and falling by two fingers, at the foot of Stopgate, on a count of four and four. That's the pumps' stroke. I measured it against a mark on the brick, with a lamp, last night, and again at six this morning with Mr. Rusk beside me."

"I saw it," said Rusk. "I don't dispute that it moves."

"Then here's what I think, and I'll say plainly that I only think it. Three pumps that used to take their stroke each in its own time now take it together. So the whole main draws and lets go like a lung. Every drum house on that line has had the same slow push come up through its floor for eleven days." Her voice did not alter. "I don't say that water can step a cell. Nobody's ever seen it. I say that the drums have fallen in exactly in the order that the water reaches them, and not in the order of the copper, and that I've one drum to prove it by."

"One," said Rusk. He said it without unkindness.

"One. If you quench two drums and I'm wrong, you've stopped it, and I'll come and say so in this room. If you quench two and I'm right, it won't know that they're dry. It'll go underneath."

"Mr. Adlam," said Ysra. "You've a patent."

"I've a patent in which no drop of water is mentioned from the first leaf to the last, ma'am." He had his hat on his knee, and he turned it. "I should be astonished if a storm drain could do what the Works spent forty years learning to do with a pinch of salt. I've been astonished five times since the spring. I've stopped regarding it as evidence." He looked along the table at Mara, quite pleasantly. "If the lady will put a date to it, I shall be very glad to be astonished a sixth."

"I'll put a date to it. I'd like it sealed, by this office, before anybody's quenched anything. I've seen what becomes of a thing that's remembered afterward."

She had written it already. She had done it at the kitchen table at one in the morning, on a half sheet, and Kiva had watched her blot it. She passed it up.

Ysra read it aloud before she folded it.

"*If it is the drain: drum twenty-eight, on Monday the third of next month, with two dry drums between. If it is the copper or the ash: nothing, and I am wrong. M. Rowan, Grade Two.*" The Chair reached for her wax. "That's five days. You'll both be very tired of one another."

"We were that by Thursday last," said Rusk. "I'll quench tonight. I'd be a fool not to. It's cheap, and it mends, and if she's wrong I've saved a school." He sat down. "And if she's right I've put six hundred families on boxes for nothing, and I shall have that to carry. I'd sooner carry it than not try."

It was a fair thing to say. Kiva could feel how fair it was, as she had once felt the fairness of an offer for a bag.

She had a question. She had had it since Monday, in a doorway, and it was not hers to ask in that room. She leaned forward and said it into the back of her aunt's collar.

Mara's head turned a quarter of an inch.

"My clerk wants to know what the shutdown is," she said. "It's a good question, and I ought to have asked it myself. You've a firebreak, Mr. Rusk. That's a fence. If the thing comes through the fence and reaches the head of that hill, what do you pull?"

"The core scrams," said the keeper, Ansty. It was the first thing that she had said. "On its own weight. I've a red lever with a pin in it, and I've pulled it twice in a drill and never in earnest. The rods fall. There's nothing in my house that needs ash to make it safe."

"So the School goes dark and nobody's hurt."

"The School goes onto boxes. I'd have the first-years sent home, all the same. I don't like them near anything that I haven't a lever for."

"Nor do I," said Rusk. "And it isn't the core that keeps me awake. It's the pumps. I've a bank of four-and-twenty that was built so that any one could fail alone. Now none of them can do anything alone. If one's fouled, I think they all cook. Then there's no stroke at all, in a wet autumn, and the Hythe has water in its cellars in half an hour." He rubbed his eyes with a thumb. "I can't quench that bank and fill it fresh. By the book it's one crew and a cell at a time, and I'd want four hours with the pumps standing. I've been given the rainfall for the month."

"There's a third thing," said Vane.

He had not moved. He had his hat on the table in front of him.

"A bank fell into step on the seventeenth, and in the same minute four husks stood a hold over a tear in the Pit. I don't say that the one made the other. I've a catcher who's taught me to be particular." He did not look at the wall bench. "I say that I had three bells on Monday and no tear under any of them. I say that every tear this spring has opened by water. The culverts. The flats. The weir. The Sag. I've a main drain breathing under a hill with a school at the top, and I don't know what a tear does when it finds the ground already keeping time."

"Nor do I," said Rhea Sorn. "I'd as soon not learn it with seven hundred children watching."

"Then I'll say what's mine," said Ysra, "and it's small." She drew a sheet toward her. "The School sends its first-years home from Friday. The rest stay; it's ten days, and I'll not empty a hill on one drum. Officer Vane will draw me a plan to clear that school and that street in the dark, by litter where it's wanted, and it will be walked on Saturday night by everybody who'd have to do it. Every four that holds a second step turns out under its officer. I want to know before I need it whether the thing can be done."

"I've never drilled a street," said the bursar faintly.

"No. You'll find that it's very like a lane, with more people in the way."

---

Four of them signed the book that evening, and there were five at the Rowan kitchen table, and a sixth who would not sit.

Jude had come for his terms. He stood by the dresser, as he had on Sunday. Pru had come since she had been told to, and had been given the stool.

Kellan had the page. He had ruled it himself.

"*In hand: held, 5.62. Credited from the Pit, 18.0. Total, 23.62.*" He looked up. "The reserve's asking every four on the roll to lend at par, for the boxes on the two dry drums. It's a loan. It comes back when the drums are filled. They've put no figure on it."

"Then we put one," said Perrin. "Four drams."

They looked at him.

"I've been on a box. In the spring, when the Pellow's core went, my aunt on Weighbridge Street had the baby's milk on a biscuit tin till noon. It isn't a hardship. It's only that you lie awake and listen for it to stop." He had gone red. "I'm the one who said *no* to six-tenths in the seventh month. So when I say four, it's four."

"Four, then," said Anwen. "I'll not argue with a man who's found his brake." She wrote it. "Call reserve. Half a dram a fighter, on the card, for as long as the bells are doubtful. That's five."

"Five?" said Jude.

"You, if you'll stand. By name, on the line, with a head's share of anything that's tinned. Those were your three things. I've not forgotten the third; she sets the mark for the blade she's got."

"There are two blades," said Jude. He did not look at Kellan.

"There are," said Kellan. "I've thought about that. Talley's the Kill. He was asked, and he stood the Regional, and I went and wore somebody else's jacket. I'm on this book as a blade who's told where he's wanted." His ears had gone white. "I'd sooner say it than have it settled round me."

Jude scratched his jaw.

"I come down heavy," he said. "You come down like a cat. There's work for a cat that I'd make a mess of, and I can think of a paving stone or two that'd turn your edge. I'd say we ask the catcher, each time, and neither of us sulks."

"I don't sulk."

"You went a week without speaking in the fifth month."

"That was thinking."

"It looked a good deal like sulking from the bench."

Kiva had been holding the page flat for Anwen with the heel of her hand. She had a thing to say. She had found it on the way back from the weir, in a van, watching a first-year cross a cord thirty times out of thirty-four.

It was not a comfortable thing. It had no shine on it at all.

"I want a line put in," she said. "A second sheet. Poles and a cord. Three drams, at the office price."

"We've a sheet," said Perrin.

"We've one. And one catcher, with eight fingers, who's entered for recovery and nothing else." She kept her eyes on the green cover. "If I'm ever told to do the other thing again, I'm not at a hem. I'm four paces off with a hand that's gone. There's a rung coming down all the same, and nobody kneeling to it. I don't want to find that out on a floor. I want Pru to have a sheet. Not mine, lent. Hers."

Nobody spoke.

Pru had gone the color of a brick.

"I cross it right thirty times in thirty-four," she said. "Pike counted. He says that's four lost bags."

"He says it's four where you were thinking. I heard him." Kiva made herself look round. "I can't do the third turn at all. I shan't ever. So you're better at it than I am now, by thirty. Write that down while it's still only arithmetic."

There was a short silence. Then Anwen laughed, once, through her nose.

"That's the first time you've ever asked for a thing for somebody else that wasn't really for you," she said. "I looked. It isn't." She dipped the pen. "*Second purse sheet and poles: 3.0. Finch to catch when Rowan is otherwise seated.* Pru. You'll want to tell Sorn. It changes her card."

"She'll have to underline me," said Pru. She said it in a very small voice, and then she sat on her hands.

Kellan read the whole of it when it was done.

"*Call reserve, half a dram a fighter, five: 2.5. Second purse sheet and poles: 3.0. Lent at par to the casualty reserve, for the boxes of the streets on dry drums, to be repaid: 4.0. Held: 14.12. Sealed, medical, W: 2.0. Not opened.*" He blotted it. "That adds."

He signed. Then he sat for a moment with the pen in his hand.

"I've answered Birch," he said. "This afternoon. I said I'd take the yard. Tuesdays and Thursdays, the contained string, nothing owed." He did not look up. "Nobody told me to. One of you tried, and took it back, and I've been rather grateful. I worked it out. I'm a better blade for that yard. I turned my back on it once in front of eleven hundred people, and I'd do it again tomorrow, and I told him so in the same letter."

"What did he say?"

"He hasn't. He doesn't write. He'll look at my feet on Tuesday and I shall know."

"That's handsome of the Works," said Perrin.

"It isn't the Works. It's Birch. I've begun to be able to tell them apart." Kellan pushed the book along. "Sign, Cade. You've a plate tomorrow. I'm told that you're passed over your head."

---

The Hall's plate lay on the Long Terrace at Stonehand on Thursday afternoon, and Perrin Cade walked round it twice before he would look at it.

It was not much to see. It was a slab of grey iron the size of a door, with two brass rings let into it, and a figure cut in the stone of the terrace beside it. There had been one figure for thirty-one years. Since the spring there had been three more, each newer and sharper than the last. *340. 350. 355. 362.*

"I was withdrawn," he said. "On the fourth of the fifth month. I'd my ribs in linen. I sat in the front row and watched three people I mess with beat a thing that had stood since my father was courting." He rolled his shoulders. "I've had five months to mind that."

"What do you want it to be?" said Kiva.

She had his coat. She had been given it, without a word, as a thing that wanted two arms and no fingers.

"I want it to be thirty counts. That's the Hall's second team. That's a place of my own, in my own colors, with nobody's brackets on it." He grinned, rather whitely. "I've been told not to want the other."

There were a dozen people on the terrace. The Hall's master was there, with his chisel in his apron. Lowen Brask stood at the rail with his notebook shut. Noa had come, with a pencil, and stood where he would be in nobody's way.

A healer strapped Perrin's wrists. The plate was loaded, slab by slab, by two third-years with a hoist, and a clerk called each weight as it went on.

"Three hundred and forty."

Perrin stood over it. He set his feet. The oak of the terrace hummed.

He lifted.

He did it as he had carried a girl across the Pit, without hurry and with his whole attention. It came up to his thighs, and his chest, and then he turned his hands under it and it went over his head, and stood there on his two locked arms like a roof.

"One," said the clerk. "Two."

Kiva counted with him. She could not help it. She watched the big red face go still, and stiller, and she saw that he was not straining. He was listening to something, as a man listens to his own ribs.

"Twenty-nine. Thirty. *Hold made.*"

He did not put it down.

"Cade," said the master.

"I'd like forty on," said Perrin. His voice came out squeezed, and perfectly polite. "If it's allowed. I'm up here now."

The master looked at the healer. The healer looked at Perrin's wrists, and his color, and nodded once. The third-years ran the hoist. It was done with the plate in the air, a slab on each side, as a man is handed two more parcels.

"Three hundred and eighty."

Something happened to the terrace. Kiva felt it in her boots. A still door that had been open went a great deal wider, quietly, like a gate swung back against a wall.

"One," said the clerk. He had lost his clerk's voice. "Two."

It was the longest thirty that she had ever counted. At twenty his arms began to shake. At twenty-five the shaking stopped, which was worse. He had his eyes shut.

"Thirty!"

He gave it to the ground. He did not drop it. He let it down through himself and into the oak, by its corners, and the Long Terrace boomed once from end to end.

Then he sat down on the plate and put his head between his knees.

Nobody cheered at first. The master had taken the chisel out of his apron and was looking at it.

"That's eighteen kilos over the best that this Hall ever saw," he said. "By a first-year. With two ribs that were in pieces at midsummer." He knelt, stiffly, at the edge of the stone. "I've cut that line four times since the thaw. I cut it once in thirty-one years before that. I said it was a strong year. I said it in the spring and I said it in the summer. I'm getting tired of hearing myself."

He began to cut.

Noa was writing. Kiva saw what he wrote, since he had turned the page a little toward her. It was his own page, the one with the heading that he had found on a tape's margin. He put the figure under four others, and the date, and nothing else.
