"That's four," he said, under his breath. "On one plate. In one year." He put the pencil behind his ear. "I've a column of those now. Hold. Draw. A plate. And I've another, for tears, and skins, and a thing that stood a square. They both go up. They've both gone up faster since the fifth month than they did before it."

"Is that a reason?"

"No. It's two columns." He looked at her. "Adlam says if I'd a third, from somewhere that wasn't Meridian, somebody would have to read it."

Perrin had lifted his head.

"I'd like it known," he said hoarsely, "that I don't mind. Not any of it. I've looked." He wiped his face on his forearm. "Is that my coat? Give it here. I'm going to go and tell my dad, and he's going to cry on a tram."

---

Drum twenty-six did not fall in on Thursday night. There was nothing in it to fall.

They had quenched it at midnight on Wednesday, and twenty-seven after it, and the Works had hung a tag on each green door. Six hundred households, in the streets that the two drums fed, had gone onto their boxes. The casualty reserve had sent a cart round with cans.

At ten that night, when the hour for it had gone by, a clerk chalked one word on the board outside the Measure Hall. *Held.*

There were people who cheered. Kiva heard them from the tram.

She went up the hill with Mara at eleven, since Mara meant to see for herself. They stopped at the rail of twenty-five, where the needle nodded, and at twenty-six, where a needle lay against its pin behind a dark glass and did nothing.

"Well, it's dry," said Mara. "I'll say that for him. He's quenched it to the bone."

Kiva had gone out into the road.

The grating was there, between the rails. The water drew in under it, and let go. She did not need to kneel. She walked on up the hill, past twenty-seven, with her hand trailing along the wet iron rail of each area as she went, until she came to the next green door.

Twenty-eight. The needle stood on its own time. She could see it through the glass.

She laid her right palm on the paving in front of the sill.

There was a wall. There was cold stone, and the wet. And under that, so faint that she would not have sworn to it before a clerk, there was something pushing.

She put her left beside it. The heel felt it, and the last two fingers. The first two told her that there was a pavement.

"It's here already," she said. "It's very small."

"Is that a result?"

"No. It's what comes through a flagstone." She stood up. "It's a reason to look."

"So it is." Mara had the book open on the rail. She did not write anything in it. "I've a cork and a yard of cord at the shop, and a slate that can be chalked from a ladder. I shall hang a float in every grating on this hill tomorrow, with a mark beside it. Then we'll have a figure for each, twice a day, in my hand and Rusk's." She looked down the long wet street, to where a word was chalked on a board a mile off. "I'd be glad to be wrong. I want you to know that. I've lain awake hoping for it."

"I know."

"I shan't be, though. Shall I?"

Kiva did not answer. It was not a thing that she could weigh.

They walked down together. Behind them, between the rails, the hill went on breathing.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-42.md

# Chapter 42 — Kiva: The Ninth Post

Healer Aske saw the hand on Friday at seven, and for the first time since the spring he did not take out the pin.

"There's no use my pricking those two," he said. "I know what they'll say. I'll look at the rest." He turned the hand over under the lamp, and pressed the palm, and the root of the thumb, and each of the last two fingers in turn. "When. When. When. Good. That's a hand with eight parts and it's had a fortnight, all but a day."

"So I'm passed."

"For what?" He let go. "You're passed to catch. You always were. You're passed for one door at a time, dark hands, from this morning; a step, a stance, a ward for a tap. I'm told that you test the ground before you'll stand on it. I approve of that." He took a fresh card, and wrote. "From tomorrow, the first, the fortnight's up. So I've to write something about the other, and I've thought about what."

He wrote for some time. Then he read it to her, as he read everything.

"*A crossing, one, in seven days, as before. The longer thing: not on my card. If somebody with a slate means to direct it, he sends for me and I say the price to his face, before, with the candidate listening. I'll not have it read off a card that I wrote in a quiet room.*" He capped the pen. "I don't know what the price is now. That's the truth of it. The two fingers have nothing left to lose. It took them first because they'd been hurt first. I don't know what it takes second."

"You could guess."

"I could. I shan't, at seven in the morning, to a girl who'd write it in a book." He gave her the card. "I'd as soon that nobody sent for me. I've no curiosity in the matter whatever."

She put the card in her tunic, with the other.

---

Mara had forty corks in a string bag, and she went up Stopgate with them like a woman delivering eggs.

It was a clear cold morning. The hill was quieter than it ought to have been. The two dry drums had six hundred households on their boxes, most of them in the streets behind. On the hill itself it was the middle stretch, and one could tell which houses by the windows, which had the dim brownish look of lamps turned down to save a can. A cart from the casualty reserve stood at the rail of the twenty-sixth drum house with its tailboard down.

"Hold that," said Mara.

It was the slate. Kiva held it against her chest with her left forearm and chalked with her right.

They did it the same way at each grating. Mara knelt in the road between the tram rails, and lowered a cork on a measured cord until it floated, and tied the cord off to the bars. Then she let down the lamp and scratched a line on the wet brick with a nail on a stick, at the place to which the water rose, and another at the place to which it fell.

"Twenty-five. Rise and fall, two fingers and a half."

Kiva chalked it.

"It was two on Tuesday."

"So it was. Write the Tuesday figure beside it, in a ring, and don't join them with anything. I'll join them when I've a third."

They went on up. At twenty-six, under a dry drum, the water rose and fell by two fingers. At twenty-seven it was one and a half. A tram went by them, clanging, and the motorman leaned out to look at a Grade Two operator on her knees in his road.

"I'd a thought in the night," said Mara, at the twenty-eighth. She was a little out of breath. "I'll tell it you, since you'll not make more of it than it is. If I'm right, and the water's doing it, then every one of these holes is a place where what's underneath can get at what's on top. I don't like a hole. I shall ask Rusk for planks."

"For forty gratings?"

"For every one on this hill, and sandbags to weight them. It'll flood the gutters if it rains. I'd sooner have wet boots." She let the cork down. "A finger. And a bit. What did your flagstone say on Thursday?"

"That something was pushing. Very small."

"It's got a figure now. I'm obliged to it."

Twenty-nine was near the top, where the houses grew taller and had brass on their doors. Half a finger. Above that there was only the last grating, at the very head of the street, and beyond it the wall.

Kiva had come up that hill every morning for six months and had never properly looked at where it ended. Stopgate did not turn, or open into a square. It simply stopped, against thirty feet of old dark brick. The tram line bent away to the east along the foot of the wall. In the middle of the wall was a cart gate of black oak, with a wicket in it, and over the gate a stone with the School's device, which was a line with a kink in it.

"A hair," said Mara, at the last grating. "I can see that it moves. I couldn't put a rule to it." She got up, with a hand on the rail. "Now. By the Works' plan the main goes on under that gate, straight as a pipe, across the bottom yard and up the School's hill. There's a rain gully in the yard that sits right over it. I want a cork in that gully."

The wicket was open. There was a short woman standing in it with a ring of keys at her belt.

"I thought you'd be up," said the keeper, Ansty. "I've had the kettle on since eight. You'll want the gully by the cart shed. Mind the trunks."

There were a great many trunks.

The bottom yard was a cobbled square, thirty paces each way, and that morning it was full of first-years going home. They sat on boxes and stood about in knots, in their walking coats, with the look of people who have been told that they are not in trouble. A carrier's dray was being loaded at the gate.

Kiva knew the yard as she knew her own stair. On the east side stood the core house, square and windowless, with its one iron door and the hum coming off it like warmth off a stove. The Low Lane was built against its south wall. Behind it, to the north, was the long slate roof of the New Lane. On the west side there was only a low wall with a gap in it, and through the gap one could see packed dirt, and a shed, and the end of a row of black posts.

"There," said Ansty.

The gully was an iron grid in the cobbles, ten paces from the core house wall. Mara knelt at it. She let the cork down.

She let it down a long way.

"That's dry," she said.

"It's a rain gully. It's dry when it isn't raining."

"It's on the main. There ought to be a foot of water under it, summer and winter, and that water ought to be moving a hair." Mara drew the cork up. It was not so much as damp. She looked at it, and then at the plan, and then at the black gate behind her, through which a thing that rose and fell was supposed to have come. "Mrs. Ansty. Where does the water in this yard go?"

"Down that. And then away."

"Away where?"

"I've never asked it," said Ansty. "I keep a core. I'm told there's a man at the Works who keeps drains."

Kiva had left them. She had the slate under her arm, and nothing to chalk.

She had not meant to walk anywhere in particular. She found that she had gone through the gap in the low wall and was standing at the east end of the Old Yard, in daylight, which she had hardly ever done.

It looked smaller by day. It was a long strip of packed ground under the bottom wall, forty paces from the gap to the sheds at the far end, with two rows of black posts down it. Somebody's scraped line was there still. So was the cross, four paces beyond it, scratched over and over in the dirt.

And the last post of the south row leaned over the cross, as it always had.

"Why's that one crooked?"

Kiva looked round.

It was a child. She was perhaps thirteen, small and sandy, with a plait and a junior's grey smock, and she was carrying a quench box in both arms that was plainly not hers. She had the fixed, careful look of somebody who has been Kindled since the spring and has not yet set anything on fire.

"I'm to take this to the shed," said the child. "They're sending the first-years home and nobody's told the juniors anything. I've been coming to this yard since the fifth month. All the others are straight. That one isn't. I asked Miss Dane, and she said it wasn't in the manual anymore."

"She says that to everybody."

"Do you know?"

Kiva considered the post. It was black, and split down one side, and bound with two iron bands. It stood a full pace out of its row, and it leaned into the lane: to the left, if one stood at the sheds and looked along the row.

She had put her hand on it when she was thirteen. She had come down beside it, with nothing open, and paid a quarter of a dram each time. She had never once been able to answer that question.

"No," she said. "I don't. I've wondered for four years."

"Oh." The child shifted the box. She seemed, if anything, relieved. "I thought it was only that I was new."

She went on down the lane toward the sheds. Kiva watched her go round the leaning post without looking at it, as everybody did, by a little sideways step that she had plainly learned in her first week.

---

Pike had them in the Graywater hall yard that evening, and it was the worst hour that Kiva had spent on a sheet since the spring.

The new one had come up from the ash office at four. It was stiff, and it smelled of wax, and Pru had carried it the whole length of the Brickfields road on her shoulder rather than put it on Perrin's handcart.

"Finch catches," said Pike. "Rowan, you're a corner."

"Which?"

"That's hers to say. You'll have noticed that it's her cloth."

Pru stood in the middle of the yard with the sheet at her feet and her round face perfectly white.

"North-east," she said. "Please. I mean, north-east. Rowan."

Kiva went to the north-east corner. She knelt, and took the hem.

It was a strange place to be. She had been the one in the middle since the fifth month. She had set the mark and thrown the cloth and called the count, and four people had done as she said. Now she had a lead weight in her right hand and the heel of her left on a hem, and a first-year behind her was reading the ground.

"Cade's the rung," said Pike. "Gerald, I should say. Off the hoist, from the height of a man. When he lands, he's sixty drams and he's open, and you've sixty counts. Finch."

"Mark," said Pru. Her voice shook. "Here. A pace south, for the wind. There isn't any wind. I'm saying it for practice." She threw the sheet.

It did not lie flat. One corner had folded under.

"Again," said Pike.

She threw it nine times. On the ninth it lay like a tablecloth, and Perrin let Gerald go from the hoist, and a hundredweight of sand hit the middle of the canvas with a sound like a dropped mattress.

"*Dark!*" said Pru. It came out as a squeak. "One! Corners!"

Kiva hauled.

She did it as she had done it a hundred times. She brought her corner in toward the heap, low, with her right hand, and she felt for the slack of the hem along her left side to tell her when the next corner was coming up to meet her.

There was no slack. There was nothing there at all. Her two fingers lay on the canvas and told her that there was canvas.

"*Rowan!*" That was Pru. "Your left! It's proud! It's standing up like a tent!"

She looked. Along her left side the hem had lifted off the ground in a long loose fold, a hand high, where a corner that had not waited for its neighbor had pulled the cloth up. Anything open would have run out under it.

"That's a lost rung," said Pike. He did not raise his voice. "Not hers. Yours. What happened?"

"I felt for the hem. On the left."

"With what?"

Kiva did not answer.

"With what, Rowan?"

"With two fingers that can't tell me there's a hem."

"So you asked a thing of a hand that it hasn't got, and you didn't say so to your catcher, and she found it out with the bag open." Pike squatted down beside her, heavily. He did not look angry. He looked like a man checking a knot. "I told you that you'd hate it for a year. This is what the year's made of. You knew about the cord, since that's the one everybody's watching. What else is there? Say them. All of them. To her, not to me."

It was not a comfortable minute. She knelt in a hall yard, in front of four people and a sandbag, and went through her own left hand like a clerk going through a drawer.

"The cord. All three turns. That's yours already." She made herself look at Pru and not at the ground. "The slack of a hem on my left side. I can't feel it come. So I want a right-hand corner, every time, where the slack's on my good side. Or somebody on my left who calls it to me."

"North-west, then," said Pru. "Or south-east. I'll write it on my hand."

"Tin lids. The cold-room kind, with the lip. I can't find the lip." She thought. "The pin of a seal. Whether wax has gone off. I'd have to look, and at night I can't."

"Wax is mine," said Anwen. She was sitting on the handcart with the green book shut on her knee. "I've done wax since I was eight. It's laundry."

"And the count of a thing." Kiva had not known that she was going to say it. "If a thing's keeping time, a hem or a cord, I used to feel it in both hands and take the middle of the two. I've only the one now that hears properly. I got the hold's breath at the Pit because Brask called it for me. I'd not have had it by myself."

Pike's square head came up a little.

"That's the one I was waiting for," he said. "I'd not have thought you'd find it tonight. Who has that?"

"I don't know. Nobody. Brask, if he's there."

"*If he's there* isn't a name. Find one. Before you want it." He stood. "Finch. Throw it again. Rowan, south-east. And this time, when you want her corner in, you tell her so. You've a voice. I've heard it called shrill."

"I'm not shrill," said Pru. "I'm carrying."

They threw it until the lamp was lit. It grew a little less dreadful. On the fourteenth, Pru said "Rowan, in a hand" in the tone in which one asks for the salt, and Kiva brought her corner in a hand. The neck closed. Pru took the cord at her elbow and made three turns, and looked at the third, and did not think about it.

"Sealed," she said. "Fifty-one." Then she sat down on the stiff new canvas and put her face in her hands.

"That's a catcher's count," said Pike. "On her own cloth. Rowan was a corner in it and did as she was bid." He rolled the cord. "I'd not have wagered a penny on either of those in the fifth month."

Kiva's knees ached. She had been corrected eleven times by a girl who had once run from a spaniel.

She found, somewhat to her surprise, that she could bear it. It was a good deal like being a still door. One was not the thing that was happening. One was only what it stood on.

---

Hollis Vane walked his plan on Saturday night, and he began it at ten with every lamp on Stopgate turned down.

"I'll say the reason for the dark," he said. He had the wardens at the foot of the hill, eleven of them with their handbells, and behind them the fours. "If a tear opens on this street, I'll want one thing lit on it, and that's a gate. Everything else that's bright is a thing for it to walk to. So you'll learn the road with your feet. There's a white tape up the east curb. There'll be a voice at every corner."

There were sixty-two houses on the hill itself. The rest of the dry drums' six hundred households lay in the streets to either side, and had roads of their own. He had the list. Fourteen had somebody in them who could not walk. For tonight a Circuit dummy lay in each of those fourteen beds, by leave of the household, and in nine of the households the family had stayed up to watch.

"The bottom forty go down. To the Brickfields road, and the tram shed, where the Wren House has its carts. The top twenty-two can't come down past whatever's in the road. They go up. Through the School's gate, across the yard, along the old practice lane under the wall, and out by the side gate to the square." He looked at the bursar. "I've been shown it by daylight. I'm told that it's the shortest way off that hill that doesn't cross a drain."

"It doesn't cross the main at all," said Mara. She was there for Rusk, with the plan. "If the main's where it's drawn."

"Is it?"

"Ask me tomorrow."

The gate stood at the head of the street, a hundred paces below the wall, silver from curb to curb. It was Doran Mace's. He had brought six of the night van's wards up from the Brickfields road and four students to stand among them, and he walked behind the line with his board under his arm, looking at boots.

Anwen was at the end of it.

Kiva saw that from where she stood. It was the east end, with the curb on one side of her and Tibb on the other, and nothing beyond her but an area railing. She had her hands low and her face shut, and the silver between them did not flicker.

"Rowan," said Vane. "You're recovery. So you've no litter and no wall. I've a use for somebody who reads ground. You'll stand in the School's yard, at the turn, and tell me what goes wrong there. Not mend it. Tell me."

"Yes, sir."

"Finch has the lamp, shuttered. Bexley's on the School's mast, on the core house roof, with his eyes on a drum and not on a bell. If that bell rings and he doesn't shout, it's the mark and not the drum, and nobody's to stop walking." He raised his voice. "*Carry! On the wardens' bells! Go!*"

Eleven handbells went off down the hill like a dropped tray.

She stood in the gap of the low wall, with Pru beside her, and listened to a street being emptied in the dark.

It was a queer, slow, shuffling noise. There were doors, and feet on steps, and a great many people saying *mind* to one another. Somewhere a child was laughing, since nobody had told it not to. A warden's voice went along the top of the hill, cracked and steady. *Odd numbers, by the tape. Evens, wait for your bell.*

The first litter came through the black gate at a trot.

It was Pettit at the head and a Stonehand first-year at the foot, and a dummy between them in a blanket. They had been told the road. Across the cobbles, through the gap, along the lane.

"Through here," said Kiva. "Lane's ahead. Posts both sides."

"I've got it," said Pettit.

He had not got it.

He went through the gap fast, with his head down, and he did what anybody does who is carrying a weight along a wall in the dark. He kept to the wall. The south row of posts stood in its line, a yard out from the brick, and he meant to run along the lane side of it as a man runs along a fence.

The nearest post of that row was not in the line. It was the last of the row, as the lane was counted from the sheds, and so the first that anybody met from the gap. It stood a pace out, eight paces in, leaning into the road at the height of a man's hip.

There was a sound like an axe going into a log.

"*Down!*" said Pettit, from the ground, in a voice of pure surprise. The dummy had gone over his head. The first-year at the foot had sat down on it. "Who put a post there? There's a post in the road!"

"It's always been there," said Pru.

"Then it's always been in the road!"

He had it on the shin. He got up, swearing in a careful undertone like a boy who has been taught not to, and they gathered the dummy and went on, limping. Kiva counted. It had cost them forty counts.

"That's one," she said.

The second litter was two third-years of the School, who had walked past that post for three years. They went round it without a check. The third was Old Four's.

Lowen Brask had the head of it. He did not trot. He came through the gap at his long, even walk, with the dummy lying in the poles as though it were asleep, and he stopped.

He stopped dead, two paces short of the post, and stood there.

"There's something here," he said, to the dark. "I can hear the wall's come nearer. Wace, a pace right. Slowly." They went round. "Rowan. Is that yours to tell?"

"It's a post. It's out of its row. It leans."

"I know which it is. I've fought in this yard." He was already past. "I'd not thought of it with a litter. Somebody ought to say so at the gap."

"I did say posts."

"You said posts both sides. That's true of all of them." His voice came back along the lane, quite without malice. "It's the one that isn't like the others that wants saying."

The fourth litter hit it.

It was two Wren House second-years with a real person, an old man from number fifty-eight who had declined to be a dummy and insisted on being carried in his own chair. He was not hurt. The chair's leg was. He made a great deal of noise about it, and a bird in a cage on his knee made more.

"Two," said Kiva. She had her book out, on her forearm, and she was writing in it by the edge of Pru's shutter. "Forty counts, and then about ninety. That's two in four, and the two that didn't were people who knew."

"You could stand at it," said Pru. "With the lamp."

"He said nothing lit."

"Then you could just stand at it. And say."

It was what she wanted to do. It was exactly what she had done at the Pit, and on a street, and in the lane on her first morning. She would plant herself where the trouble was and call it for everybody, in a carrying voice, until the last litter was through. She had her mouth open to say *I'll take it.*

*Tell me what goes wrong. Not mend it.*

She shut her mouth.

It was not that she could not do it. It was that if she stood at that post, then the post was hers, and on the night that mattered she would be somewhere else. She would be four paces off with a hand gone, as she had told a kitchen table on Wednesday, and there would be a post in the road and nobody at it.

"No," she said slowly. "It's not a thing for one person to stand at. It's a thing for whoever's carrying to say."

The fifth litter was Perrin's.

She heard him before she saw him. He was telling the dummy that it was being rescued.

"Perrin! Stop at the gap!"

He stopped. He had Hendry Moss of his own Hall behind him, a boy with a neck as thick as his head, and they stood there in the dark with the poles on their shoulders.

"There's a post," said Kiva. "The last of the south row. It's a pace out, into the lane, on your left as you go in. I'm not going to stand at it. I want you to call it. Aloud, before you're through the gap. *Post, left. Keep right.* And then I want Moss to say it back."

"Post, left. Keep right," said Perrin obediently.

"Post left, keep right," said Moss.

"And when you're past it, you say it over your shoulder. To whoever's behind. Whether you can see them or not."

"What if there's nobody behind?"

"Then you've told a wall, and it's cost you nothing."

Perrin thought about that.

"It's a lane call," he said. "It's *Yours* and *Mine.* It's only that it's for a post." He hitched the poles. "I call that rather good. *Post, left! Keep right!*"

They went through. They did not touch it. She heard him bawl it backward down the lane, in the voice that he had been saving since the spring, and a moment later she heard somebody at the black gate take it up who had not been told to. *Post left. Keep right.*

By the ninth litter it was coming up the hill ahead of the bearers. By the fourteenth a warden with a handbell was calling it at the wicket, and had no idea why.

She wrote that down. *Told one team. The call went on by itself.*

Then the bell rang.

It was the School's own, on the core house roof, and it rang right over their heads, two and two and two, as loud as a dray on cobbles. Every foot in the yard stopped.

It lasted perhaps three seconds. There was a litter halted in the gateway, and a warden with her bell held up and her mouth open, and forty people on a dark hill all turning their faces the same way.

"*It's the mark!*" That was Noa. His thin cracked voice came down off the roof. "*It's the mark, not the drum! I've got the needle! Walk! Go on walking!*"

"*Walk!*" said Vane, from somewhere below. "*You heard the roof! That's what you've a roof for!*"

They walked.

It was done by twenty past eleven. Vane read the time off his watch at the black gate, with the wardens round him and the fours sitting on the cobbles.

"An hour and twenty minutes. For sixty-two houses, fourteen litters and a school, with nothing chasing us." He did not sound displeased. He did not sound pleased. "I'd want it in forty. We'll walk it again on Tuesday. Now. What went wrong? Bottom of the hill first."

They told him. A warden had rung evens before odds. The tape had come off the curb at number thirty. Twenty-Eight's ward had heard a join in Mace's gate and had said so, and had been right.

"The yard," said Vane. "Rowan."

"A post," said Kiva. She stood up. "The last of the south row in the old lane. It's a pace out of line, and it leans into the road at hip height. Two litters in the first four struck it. Forty counts and ninety. The two that missed it were people who'd known it for years, so it wasn't that they saw it." She read it off the page. "Brask stopped short of it and felt his way. Then I had one team call it at the gap and call it back over the shoulder, and after that nobody struck it. I didn't stand at it. I thought I shouldn't be there to."

"A post," said Vane.

"Yes, sir."

"In the middle of my casualty road." He turned his head. "Bursar. What is it doing there?"

The thin man in the gown had been sitting on a trunk. He got up.

"It is retained," he said, "for ceremonial continuity. That's the manual's phrase. It's the oldest thing in the School. It's said to be older than the School."

"Is it holding anything up?"

"I shouldn't think so."

"Then it can be ceremonial somewhere that I'm not carrying an old man in a chair. I'll have it out by Tuesday. I've a man with a saw."

"No," said Orla Dane.

She had been in the yard the whole night, on her crate, by the first shed, where she had sat for one-and-twenty years. Nobody had asked her for anything. She had her stick between her boots, and she did not raise her voice.

"You'll not put a saw to that, Hollis. Not on a bursar's word, nor on mine."

"Give me a reason."

"I haven't one." The crooked face was grave. "That's what I've to say, and I've said it to every first-year who ever asked. There used to be one. It was printed. It was taken out of the manual seventy-seven years ago by a committee, and something civil was put in its place, and nobody now living at this School has read what was there before." She shifted the stick. "I've sat under that post since before you had a slate. I've wondered what it was for most nights. I've never once thought that the answer was *nothing.*"

"That's not a reason. That's a feeling about one."

"It is. I'm not a girl; I know the difference." Orla looked along the fours until she found the one she wanted. "You've a ledger in your hall that's older than this School. You found a hand in the back of it that had been tidied away, and you put it back, and I thought the better of you. I'm asking you to look once more before you cut. If there's nothing, I'll hold the lamp for your man myself."

Vane was silent.

He was a watch officer. He had stood on the cinders of the Pit and read a column aloud that somebody had ruled out of a book. Kiva saw him weigh the one thing against the other, a post and a litter, and she saw that he did not enjoy it.

"Tuesday," he said. "Noon. It's in the road, Dane, and a girl of seventeen has just shown me how to get forty people round it with their voices, which I'll grant you is more than the saw would have taught them. That buys it three days. Bring me why it's there, or it comes out." He put his watch away. "And I'll look in my own. I'd be a poor sort of man to ask it of your shelf and not of mine."

"Bexley," said Orla.

Noa had come down off the roof. He had a roll of tape under his arm and his pencil behind his ear, and he was already looking at the post as he looked at a notch.

"The superseded stacks," he said. "Three flights down. I know the stair. The Reader shut at six." He swallowed. "She opens at eight on a Monday. I shall be on the step."

---

It rained a little before dawn, and Mara was on the hill by seven on Sunday to see what the rain had done to her corks.

It had done nothing. A wet drain breathed exactly as a dry one had. She read them from the bottom, with Kiva chalking, and the slate filled slowly with pairs of figures, a Friday and a Sunday, each in its ring.

"Twenty-five. Two and a half, and two and a half. Six-and-twenty, two, and two. Seven, one and a half, and a little over." She knelt at the next. She was a long time. "Eight-and-twenty. It was a finger and a bit. It's a finger and a half."

"It's growing."

"It's growing at the top of the hill and standing still at the bottom. Like a tide making up a creek." Mara sat back on her heels in the road. "Tomorrow's the third. If that drum does what I wrote down, I shall be believed, and I find that I've no stomach for it."

They went on. Nine-and-twenty was a full finger. The last grating, under the wall, was more than a hair now. One could have put a rule to it.

Ansty let them in at the wicket. The yard was empty and wet, and the trunks were gone.

Mara went straight to the gully by the cart shed. She let the cork down, and drew it up, and turned it over in her fingers.

"Wet," she said. "From the rain. There's an inch of rainwater in the bottom of that, lying as flat as a plate." She did not get up. "It isn't moving. Not a hair. I've a main that breathes a rule's width on one side of that gate, and ten paces inside it I've a puddle."

"Then it isn't under here."

"It's drawn under here." She had the plan out. She laid it on the wet cobbles and put her knee on a corner of it. "There. A blue line, from the gate, north, across this yard, past the core house, and on up the hill. It's as straight as if somebody had done it with a ruler."

Kiva looked at the line. It was very neat. It had a small note beside it, in a clerk's round hand.

"*Recopied from the worn sheet, 2553,*" she read.

"So it was done with a ruler." Mara's voice was quite level. "A clerk had a sheet that was coming to pieces, and he copied it fair, and where the old line wandered he made it sensible. I've done that reconciling for eleven years. I know that hand. It's the hand of a man who thinks that he's being helpful."

She shut her eyes for a moment.

"Somebody's tidied my drain," she said.

Kiva had her palm on the cobbles already.

There was nothing. There was wet stone, and under it the dead flat silence of ground with no hollow in it. She moved a pace toward the gate, and knelt again. Nothing. A pace west. Nothing.

She worked along the inside of the bottom wall. She did it on her knees, in the wet, with her right palm and the heel of her left, as a person feels along a shelf in the dark for a thing that has rolled. Mara watched her and did not say a word.

At the gap in the low wall she felt it.

It was very small. It was the least thing that she had ever called a feeling. It came up through the packed dirt on the far side of the gap, slow and even, and it was not coming from the north.

She went through on her knees. She followed it. It was like following the warmth of a pipe along a wall. It ran west from the gate, close under the brick, and it grew a little as she went, and then, eight paces into the Old Yard, it stopped being ahead of her.

It was underneath. It was directly underneath, and stronger than anywhere, and beyond that place it went off at a slant, along the lane, toward the sheds.

She took her hands off the ground and looked up.

There was a cross in the dirt in front of her knees, scratched over and over. There was a black post beside it, split, with two iron bands.

"It's here," she said. Her voice came out oddly. "It doesn't go north. It comes in under the gate and turns. It runs along under the wall to here, and then it goes off down the lane, on the south side, about where the posts are." She sat back. "I can't weigh it. It's what comes through dirt. You'd have to dig."

"I can't dig up a school on a palm."

"No."

"I can't dig it up on a cork, either, since I've no hole to put a cork down." Mara had come to the gap. She stood looking at the lane, and the two rows of posts, and the one at the end that was a pace out of its line. "There'll be a sheet older than that plan. There always is. Somebody drew the worn one before it was worn."

"Noa's at the Library tomorrow at eight. About the post."

"Then he may ask about a drain while he's there. It's the same stair."

She went back to her gully, and her puddle, to write down what she had not found.

Kiva stayed where she was.

She had come down on that cross again and again since the summer. She had stood on it in the still door, with the whole of the Old Yard under one heel, and felt the ground take her. It had never boomed. It had never been anything but ground. Whatever was down there was a long way down, and bricked over, and it had been there for longer than anybody had kept the reason.

She looked at the post.

It leaned out over the lane. She had always thought of it as leaning away from its row, like a tooth gone bad. A child with a plait had gone round it on Friday by a little sideways step, without looking, and so had every student who had ever used that yard.

It struck her now, for the first time, that it might not be leaning away from anything. It might be leaning toward.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-43.md

# Chapter 43 — Kiva: A Drain in a Box

The Halden Works lent its license yard on Sunday afternoon, and Foreman Birch made it very plain whose yard it was.

"My slate," he said, at the rail. "My ward line. My string, that I've had in that cage since the thaw, and I know its temper. The Municipal Works has asked for a floor that keeps step, and there's only the one in Meridian with a rift on it. It's got it. Nobody lights so much as a pipe on that brick until I've said."

It was a bigger floor than the Kennel's. It was paved in blue brick, with a lattice house at the north end and a paid ward line in black standing easy along the wall. Kiva had not been inside the refinery gate before. She had counted the rivets on the way under it, since somebody had once told her the number, and had lost count at two hundred.

What Mara had built stood down the middle of the yard.

It was a trough. It was twelve paces long, knee high, made of new planks and lined with lead, and it had a foot of water in it. At the south end stood a brewer's hand pump with its handle off, and beside the pump, on a bench, six cells in a row with their needles under glass.

All six needles were nodding. The pump drew, and let go, as they did. Down the whole length of the trough the water rose a finger on the lead, and fell.

"There's your main," said Mara. "And there's your pump house. Mr. Adlam's people stepped that bank for me at eight this morning, with a pinch of their own salt, by the book. It took them four minutes. They were very civil about it."

"And that?" said Kiva.

At the north end of the trough, twelve paces from the bench, there was a stool. The stool stood with its feet in the water. On it sat a single cell with a needle of its own.

"That's a drum on Stopgate. One plain cell, filled at eight, on its own time. There isn't a wire to it. There's nothing between that cell and the bench but twelve paces of water going up and down." Mara looked at it with dislike. "At eight it stood. At ten it had a tremble. I'd like you to come to the rail and tell me what it's doing now. Not past the rail."

Kiva went to the rail.

The needle on the stool went up, and came down. It did it with the other six.

"It's in," she said.

"It went in at ten minutes to twelve. Mrs. Renn had her glass on it."

There was a woman at the bench whom Kiva had never met and knew at once. She was narrow and upright, with a grader's seal on a cord round her neck, and she had Kellan's eyes. In front of her stood a brass instrument like a clock with no hands.

"It didn't go all at once," said Kellan's mother. She did not look up from the glass. "It leaned for an hour. Then it went. I'd say that a cell is a patient sort of thing, and it's been pushed from underneath, quite gently, a great many times."

"Then it's the water," said Rusk.

He stood behind the bench with his thumbs in his waistband. He looked like a man who has slept in a chair.

"It's the water in a box," said Mara. "I'll not say more than that until tomorrow night, and nor should you. I've a paper under seal." She took a half sheet from her apron. "But I'll say what we've all been assuming, since Thursday, as though it were written in the patent. I've put it down so that it can be wrong in front of everybody."

She read it out.

"*That the water moves because the pumps move it. Stop the pumps and the main lies still. Then a bank may be quenched and filled fresh, and it will keep the time that it is given.*"

"That's the plan," said Rusk. "That's the whole of the plan, if the fence fails. I stop my three pumps for as long as the sump will bear, and I put every hand in the Works on that bank at once, one to a cell. That brings my four hours down to half of one."

"It's a good plan. I'd have drawn it myself." Mara put the half sheet down on the bench, under the brass weight that she had brought from her own counter. "I'd like to see it work once in a box before I trust a district to it. And Officer Vane wants to know what a string makes of a floor like this one. Mr. Birch has been kind enough to say that we may ask it."

"I said you might ask," said Birch. "I didn't say it'd answer politely."

There were a good many people along the rail. The Placement Chair had come, since there were students on a floor with a rift at one end of it, and she stood by the gate with her hands folded. Hollis Vane was there with his slate. Rhea Sorn had brought Pike, and Greta Stroud, and Ruddock with his bag, and nobody else.

The fours were along the east wall, dark. Old Four had come, and Twenty-Eight with its reserve blade, and Halden's own three in their black and gold. Seven stood at the end.

"Gate," said Birch.

Four of his paid wards walked out onto the blue brick. They brought a wall up twelve paces west of the trough, as the Circuit's book had required since the spring, silver and square.

"That's the brightest thing on my floor. It's lit, and it's worked, and it's nearer than the bench. Any string I ever let out of that cage has gone to a gate like a dog to its dinner." He raised his arm. "Lattice. Give me the door."

The cage at the north end opened with a rattle.

Kiva had seen a good many strings by now. This one was small. It had six knots and not eight, and it stood no higher than a tram's window, on four legs. It had frost on it from crown to knee.

It came out onto the brick and stopped. Its blunt top turned, slowly, a quarter of a circle, as a weathercock turns.

It turned past the gate.

It did not pause on it. Four people were burning ash to make the brightest thing in the yard, and the grey head went by them as a man's eyes go by a stranger's in a street. It came round to the south, and stopped, pointing down twelve paces of lead-lined trough at a bench.

"That's not right," said Birch.

The string walked. It walked to the north end of the trough and stood there, astride the stool, with one pair of legs on either side of the water. The cell on the stool went on nodding between its knees.

Then the shine on it moved. It ran down off the shoulders, as Kiva had seen it run once before, in the Pit, in the spring. Two white cords went down from the lowest knot, as thick as a wrist, and into the water.

"Sorn," said Vane.

"I see it." Rhea had not moved from the rail. "It did that on the bus, in the fifth month. I said then that it had found something better than a gate. I didn't know what the something was."

"It's found the beat," said Mara. Her voice was very quiet. "It doesn't want the bright thing. It wants the thing that keeps time."

"Then I'll have a rung off it while it's standing still to be cut," said Birch. "That's what my paper says a string in my yard is for. Renn."

---

Kellan Renn had come through the refinery gate that afternoon in his own coat, and for the first time in his life he had not counted the rivets.

He had been thinking about his feet.

Birch had not answered his letter. Birch did not write. He had been standing at the rail when the students came in, with his slate, and he had looked at each of them as a grader looks at a tin. When he came to Kellan he had looked down.

*They've not got worse,* he had said. *Tuesdays and Thursdays. Bring your own cuff.*

That was the whole of it. It was a place, and nothing was owed for it, and there was no jacket on a peg.

"Top rung," said Birch now. "Yarrow has the cloth. He kneels where you put him, and you ask him. I'm told that's how it's done by people who mean to bag anything."

"Yes, sir."

He walked out onto the blue brick, dark.

He disliked it at once. It was not the string. He had cut that string's top rung nine times since the seventh month, and he knew how it stood. It was the floor. There was something coming up through his boot soles, slow and even, like the tread of a great many people going past on the other side of a wall. His feet wanted to fall in with it.

He had the best feet in the School. He had been told so since he was fourteen. It was a new thing to find that they could be asked to do something by a floor.

*Count against it,* he thought. He had watched a girl do that on the cinders of the Pit, with a hold's corner planted under her heel. He had not understood what she was at until a blade with a broken leg had explained it to a cart.

He counted in threes. It was the first number that came. His feet steadied.

Yarrow was laying the cloth. He was a still-door, long and incurious, and he threw a sheet as a man lays a table that he has laid before. He put it on the east side of the trough, hard up against the planks, under the string's top knot.

"That's close to the water," said Kellan.

"It's where it'll fall."

"It's a pace from a thing that's got two cords down in it."

"I've sixty counts," said Yarrow, patiently. "Ash doesn't run. It lies. It lies where it lands and leans to the nearest lamp, and I've put myself between it and the bench. What else is there?"

It was a fair answer. It was what the book said. Kellan looked at the trough, and at the white cords going down into it, and could not find the place in the book where it said anything else.

He looked along the east wall instead. He did it without quite meaning to. There was a catcher there who was particular about ground.

She had her eyes on the cloth. She was frowning. But she was not his catcher today, and she had not been asked, and he saw her shut her mouth on whatever it was.

"Renn," said Birch. "While we're young."

He went up the back of it dark, on his struts, in two bounds.

"Now?"

"Not yet," said Yarrow. "It's swaying. Now."

He lit.

It was four-tenths. He had it to a hair. The edge went through the top knot level and came out the other side, and he was dark again before his feet had found the brick. He had never made a cleaner one in that yard.

Sixty drams fell like sand out of a split sack.

"*Dark! One!*"

It did not lie.

He saw it from three paces. The heap hit the canvas and spread, as a heap does, and then the near edge of it began to move. It did not drift. It ran. It went across the waxed cloth toward the trough in a grey tongue, as flour runs to the low side of a tilted board, and over the hem, and up the planks.

It was climbing the cord.

"*Corners!*" Yarrow had the hem in both fists. His voice had gone up. "Corners, *now,* it's going—"

Pettit and Hesketh hauled. The neck came in. It closed on what was left, and Yarrow's cord went round it three times as fast as Kellan had ever seen a cord go.

"Sealed," said Yarrow. "Twenty—" He stopped. He weighed the bag in his two hands, as a catcher will. "That's not thirty. That's not anything like thirty."

There was a grey scum on the water.

It lay all down the trough, from the stool to the pump, moving with the rise and fall. Half a rung of open ash had gone into a foot of water inside ten counts.

"The bench," said Kellan's mother.

She said it quite quietly. He turned his head.

All six needles had jumped. He saw them do it, together, like six hands flung up. They stood for a moment against their upper pins, quivering. Then they lay down. They did not come down one after another, as cells die in a bank. They went as one, and the glass over the third cell cracked across with a small flat sound.

The pump stopped.

"That's fouled," said Rusk. His voice was thick. "That's one pinch of open ash in the water, and six cells cooked inside a breath. All six. There wasn't one of them that could say no to the others."

"There were six," said Mara. "I've four-and-twenty."

Nobody answered her.

Kellan stood on the blue brick with his blade dark on its cuff. He had cut a rung as well as he knew how, on the word of a catcher who had done what the book said, and it had killed a bank.

*I asked,* he thought. *I asked him and he told me, and it was a good answer.*

He had learned to ask. It had cost him a season. It had not until this moment crossed his mind that asking the catcher was no use at all when the catcher did not know either.

---

"The pump's dead," said Kiva.

She had not been asked. She had said it aloud all the same, since it was the thing that was there.

"We can see that the pump's dead," said Birch.

"The water isn't."

They looked.

The brewer's pump stood with its plunger down. The six cells on the bench lay cold. Nothing in that yard was drawing on the trough, and all down its twelve paces the grey water rose a finger on the lead, and fell.

In. And out.

Mara walked the length of it. She walked slowly, with her eyes on the wet line. She stopped at the north end, where the string stood astride the stool on its four legs, with five knots left and two white cords going down between them.

The cords were moving. They drew up, a hand, and let down. Three grey knots above them leaned back a little as they did it, and eased, like three people hauling a neck shut on a called word.

"It's pumping," said Mara.

She turned round. Her face was perfectly composed, and Kiva had never seen it so white.

"There. That's my half sheet. *Stop the pumps and the main lies still.* It doesn't. If there's one of those standing in it, the pumps may stop and the bank may cook, and the water goes on keeping time, since the thing's learned the stroke and has two arms down the hole." She picked the paper off the bench. "And I'd have filled four-and-twenty cells fresh, in the dark, on the strength of that sentence."

"Fill the one," said Rusk. "The stool. Show me the rest of it. I'd sooner see the whole of what I've got wrong."

A Works fitter did it, at arm's length, with the string's legs on either side of him and a ward of Birch's between. He quenched the cell on the stool, and drew it, and slid a fresh can home.

"Open," he said. "One. Two."

At forty the needle rose and stood, on a time of its own. The fitter let out his breath.

At fifty-one it trembled.

At fifty-eight it went up, and came down, with the water.

"Inside the count," said Pike. He was looking at it as he looked at a neck. "Fresh ash is open for a slow sixty. It takes the first pattern that touches it. I've said that to every corner-hand I ever trained, about a lit blade." He shook his square head. "I never thought to say it about a puddle."

"So I can't quench it while it's in the water," said Rusk. "And I can't stop the water while it's in the water. And I can't cut it while it's in the water, or the rung runs down its own arm and cooks my bank." He counted them off on his thumbs. "Then it must come out of the water. How does one take a thing like that by the arm?"

"You don't put a ward on it," said Greta Stroud.

She said it from the rail, hoarsely, without being asked.

"I've said that on two floors now. I'll say it on every one I'm ever stood on. I put a wall against a cord like that in the spring, and I meant to keep hold. It went up me to the elbows."

"Iron, then," said Rusk. "Dead iron. Something with nothing in it to take."

They tried iron. It was a fair thing to try.

The fitter had a boat-hook. He stood well back, on the west side, and reached across the trough with the full length of it, and got the hook under the nearer cord where it left the water.

Frost ran up the shaft like a fuse.

"*Let go!*" said Rhea Sorn.

He had let go already. He was not a fool. The boat-hook hung in the air for a moment, white from end to end, with nobody holding it. Then the cord drew up, a hand, and the hook went with it and was laid down in the water as gently as a spoon.

The fitter stood looking at his glove. The palm of it had gone stiff, and cracked when he bent it.

"That's my left," he said. "And me right-handed, if anybody's writing."

"It's noted," said Perrin, with feeling, from the wall.

Nobody laughed much.

Kiva had been watching the cord.

She knew that rise and fall. She had had it in a tray between her palms, in the Pit, for one slack count, light as a clothesline. She had not held it. That was the whole point of what she had done. She had put a pan under it at the instant that it stopped pulling, and had been somewhere else before it began again.

"It's got a slack," she said. "Four counts hauling and four easing. A ward for one tap, on the slack, and folded before the haul comes back. It doesn't take a ward that isn't there any longer."

"That's a catcher's notion," said Pike.

"It's what I did. I only did it once."

"I know what you did. I was behind three posts with three cloths." He was not looking at her. He was looking along the east wall, at a small round first-year with a yellow card. "It wants the best fold in the yard. That isn't yours."

"No," said Kiva. "It's hers."

Pru Finch looked behind her, to see who was meant.

"Me?"

"You fold from the far edge. You don't let go. You've always got the last of it in your palms." Kiva heard that she was talking quickly, and slowed. "I learned it from you on a pail. I do it with two fingers stuck out like a fork. You'd do it properly."

"I can't go anywhere with it. I haven't an Ember. I can't put it down."

"Nobody's asking you to go anywhere. I'd like to know whether it can be lifted off the water at all. For a count. By a ward that isn't taken."

Pru looked at Rhea Sorn.

"Your card says a corner and a lamp," said Rhea. "And the cord. I underlined the cord on Friday." The pale eyes rested on her. "It doesn't say this. So it's yours to refuse, and I'll think none the worse. Stroud will tell you what it costs if it's late."

"Six weeks," said Stroud. "And a spoon held for you."

Pru swallowed. She put her card away in her tunic, and buttoned the pocket.

"I'd want it called," she said. "The slack. I can't watch the cord and my hands."

"I'll call it," said Kiva.

It was out before she had thought. Of course she would call it. It was a hem under load, with a count in it, and she was the catcher. She had called the count on every sheet that Seven had thrown since the fifth month.

She went and knelt at the rail of the trough, a long pace from the planks. She laid her hands on the blue brick, the right palm and the heel of the left.

It came up through the brick, slow and even. She could feel the haul in it. She could feel the easing.

Pru knelt on the east side, three paces from the string's near leg, with a ward of Birch's standing over her like a porch. She put up her round hands. The silver stood between them, no bigger than a tea tray, and she bent it into the bowl of a spoon.

"*Out,*" said Kiva. "Two. Three. Four. *In*—"

She knew as she said it.

It was late. It was not much late. It was the width of the difference between two hands, of which one told her the truth and the other told her that there was a floor. She had taken the middle of them, as she had for four years, and the middle was wrong by half a count.

Pru slid the pan under.

The cord was not slack. It was just beginning to draw. It came up into the bowl of that small bright ward with the whole haul of three knots behind it, and Kiva saw the silver go grey at the rim, like a window breathed on.

"*Fold!*" That was Pike, and Stroud, and Anwen, all at once.

Pru folded.

She did it faster than Kiva had ever seen anything done with a ward. Far edge. Near edge. A penny. Shut. The cord fell back into the water with a slap.

She sat down on the brick with her hands held up in front of her face.

They were white. They were white to the first joint, all eight fingertips and the two thumbs, as though she had dipped them in milk.

"They've gone to sleep," she said, in a high surprised voice. "They've all gone to sleep. I'm narrating. I can't feel my card. I had my card."

Ruddock was over the rail already, with a towel. Anwen was a pace behind him.

Kiva had not moved. She was still kneeling, with her hands on the brick.

"Who called that?" said Pike.

"I did."

"I know that you did. I want the rest of it."

She made herself say it to the whole yard, in a carrying voice, as she would have called a mark.

"I called the *in* half a count late. I had it through two hands and I took the middle. One of them can't hear." Her mouth was dry. "I told a yard on Friday night that I couldn't take a count any longer by myself. I said that somebody else would have to have it. Then I knelt down here and took it, since it's what I've always done. That's mine. It's her hands."

"It's the tips," said Ruddock, over his shoulder. "It's a nip. She folded inside it. Ten minutes, and she'll be able to pick her nose."

"I don't," said Pru faintly.

Kiva stood up. Her knees were not quite steady.

"Brask," she said. "You called the Pit's. Would you call this?"

Lowen Brask had been standing with Old Four, with his notebook shut. He came along the wall without hurry.

"I'd call it," he said. "I'd sooner you'd asked me five minutes ago. I was standing there with it in my mouth." He looked at her, quite kindly. "You said it on Friday in the Graywater yard, I'm told. It's all over the Hall. *The count of a thing. Somebody else has that.* It's a good rule. You'll have to keep it on the days that you're frightened as well."

"I know."

"I know you know. I'm only saying it as it was said to me." He turned to the trough. "Finch. When you're ready. Not before."

It was twelve minutes before the color came back. Pru would not be hurried, and nobody hurried her. Then she knelt down again in the same place, with her jaw set, and put up her hands.

"*Out,*" said Brask. His voice was as even as a clock. "Two. Three. Four. *In.*"

The pan went under.

For one count the white cord lay in a small silver spoon, a hand's breadth above the water, light as a clothesline.

"*Two.* Fold."

It was gone. The cord dropped.

And down the whole length of the trough, the water missed a beat.

Kiva saw it. Everybody saw it. The grey scum rose a finger on the lead, and fell, and rose, and then for the space of one slow breath it only lay there and rocked.

On the stool, between the string's knees, the single needle checked. It stood for a count. Then the water drew again, and it went on.

"There," said Kellan's mother, from her glass. "It fell out. For as long as that arm was off the water, that cell had nobody to follow. It came back in as soon as it was asked."

"Then it wants more than a tap," said Rusk slowly.

"It wants the arm kept off," said Mara. "Lifted, and taken away, and put down in dry ground, and held there. For as long as it takes a Works crew to do the rest." She did not look at the east wall. "I've seen that done. I sat in a gallery and watched it. I don't know of any other hand that can."

Nobody in the yard looked at Kiva.

It was a strange thing to be at the middle of. Forty people had all at once found something to study in the brick, or the lattice, or their own boots.

"I'll say it, since it's on my board," said Ysra Crest, from the gate. Her level voice carried the whole floor. "*It is not to be asked of her.* I wrote that under a result, with my name. I meant it. Nobody in this yard will ask."

"Nor will it be offered," said Rhea Sorn. "Not on my card."

She had turned her back on the trough. She was speaking to Vane, and to Rusk, and to a woman in a work apron, and not to a girl of seventeen.

"She's attached to me. That's my hand on her entry and my name against whatever becomes of her. I had her spend two fingers on a floor of mine three Saturdays ago, with the price read out first, and I told her afterward that I'd never once named a price before I cut." Her voice was flat. "I've named this one to myself. It's too dear. I'll not have a catcher of mine go up in the dark over a hole in the road and come down with half a hand, for a plan that had a wrong sentence in it an hour ago. Find me another way."

"I'd be glad to," said Mara. "What is it?"

"Plank the holes. Every one. Give it no water to put an arm in. Then it's a string on a street, and I take it down a rung at a time on a gate, as I've taken two hundred."

"And if it finds a hole that we didn't?"

"Then I'll cut it where it stands and bag what I can."

"With its arm down my drain. You watched what half a rung did to six cells."

"I watched."

They looked at one another. They were not angry. Kiva saw that, and it frightened her more than anger would have. They were two women who were each right about a thing, and the things did not fit.

She had an answer.

It was a good one. It was about a foreman with nobody south of a string, and a hand that was already spent, and what a catcher was for. It would have sounded very well on that brick.

She looked at Pru's fingers. They were pink again, and Pru was holding them up to the light one at a time, as though she had been given them for a present.

*I'd an answer ten minutes ago,* thought Kiva. *It was to kneel down and call it.*

She put the good one where she had put the others.

"Sorn's right that it isn't hers to have offered," she said. "I've nothing to say. I'd like to know how you get that one back in its cage."

Birch grunted.

"That's the first sensible question since two o'clock. I don't. Not with its arms in a trough." He jerked his head at the fitter. "There's a bung in the south end of that. Pull it."

The fitter pulled it.

The water went out across the blue brick in a grey sheet and found the yard's drain. The trough emptied in a slow minute. The wet line went down the lead, and down, and there was a foot of air where the water had been.

The two white cords hung in it.

They hung for perhaps ten counts, reaching, with nothing to reach. Then they simply let go. They came apart from the bottom, softly, into loose grey ash that pattered on the lead like rain.

The shine went back up onto the string's shoulders. Its blunt head lifted. It turned, a quarter of a circle, and found a wall of silver twelve paces to the west, and walked to it and leaned.

"Now it's a string," said Birch. "Now I know what to do with it."

"You took the water away," said Kiva.

"I pulled a bung. I can't pull a bung on the Ferren main."

"No." Mara had bent over the empty trough. She was looking at the lead, and the wet, and the little heap of grey sweepings where two arms had been. "No, nobody can drain a main. But one might shut a piece of it. A sluice. A gate, let down across the brick, with the hill on one side and the low streets on the other. They had them on every drain in this city before there were pumps." She straightened. "I've never seen one on that plan."

"The plan's been tidied," said Kiva.

"So it has."

"Why is it called Stopgate?"

She had not known that she was going to ask it. It had been lying in her head since Tuesday, with a great many other things that she did not know the use of.

Mara opened her mouth. She shut it again.

"I've no idea," she said. "I've walked up it for thirty years. I supposed that it was somebody's name."

---

Noa was on the Library step at eight on Monday, as he had said, and Kiva was beside him.

The Reader let them in herself. She was small and upright, with two pairs of spectacles, and she looked at Kiva as one looks at a borrower who has brought a book back clean.

"The post," she said. "I've been waiting for somebody from that School to ask me since 2649. I'd begun to think that I should have to put it in my will." She took a lamp from its hook. "Three flights. Mind your heads on the second."

The superseded stacks smelled of cold iron and paste. She went along a row without reading the labels and drew out a thick grey volume with a broken spine.

"The *Standard Lane Manual,* eleventh edition. Withdrawn 2603. There's the lane."

It was halfway down a page. It had the exercise set out in six ruled columns, in print, and a seventh squeezed in at the right.

*The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road isn't straight.*

Beside it, in pencil, in a very small hand, somebody had written a line. *Retired from standard ed., 2603. Committee vote 11–6. Original reason retained here.*

"That's a junior archivist," said the Reader. "She'd no leave to write in it. I've blessed her for it rather often."

"*Because the road isn't straight,*" said Noa. He read it twice, with his lips. "That isn't a reason. It's—"

"A riddle. So the committee said. It's in the minutes." The Reader turned back a leaf. "Now read me the other six columns."

Kiva read them. The first was only the lane's name, and where it lay. *What:* nine posts in two rows. *How:* run dark, on a count. *Went wrong:* a collision at the ninth. *Cost.* *Second try.* It was all there, tidy and complete.

"It says posts," she said slowly. "*The lane with the crooked posts.* There's only the one."

"Is there?" The Reader looked at her over the upper spectacles. "In the eleventh edition there's a plate. I'll show you."

It was an engraving, brown with age. It showed a strip of ground under a wall, with two rows of posts down it, and not one of them stood square to its neighbor. The whole lane curved. It bent like a river, from the near end to the far, and the last post of the south row stood a pace out from all the rest with something drawn across its top.

"That's a bar," said Noa. "Through the post. Like a capstan."

"The School straightened the lane in 2390, when it built the wall higher. It's in the bursar's accounts. *To setting the posts fair, eleven days.* They couldn't shift the last one. It doesn't say why." She shut the book. "And that's all that my shelf can give you. A plate, and a riddle, and a clerk's pencil. I can tell you that a reason was there. I can't tell you what it was. This is the manual. It kept the why and lost the what."

Somebody was coming down the iron stair.

He came at a walk. He had his hat in one hand and a long flat tin case under his arm.

"Then I've the other half," said Hollis Vane. "I was until two this morning finding it. It was in the chest under the bearer's box, with the Line's lane sheets, which nobody has unrolled since my grandfather was a boy." He laid the case on the reading shelf. "It's in the record hand. I can't read a word of it. I've brought it to the only person in Meridian who can."

The Reader put on the second spectacles over the first.

It was a single sheet, soft as cloth, ruled in seven columns. She bent over it for some time.

"This is older than my manual," she said. "It's a copy, in the record hand, of something a good deal older again. It's the same entry. It isn't the same words." She read it out, slowly, a column at a time.

"*The lane with the crooked posts. At Meridian, under the south wall, on the line of the hill drain.*

"*What: nine posts, set as the drain runs, which is not straight. The ninth is the sluice post. It stands out from the wall for the bar.*

"*How: run it dark, from the sheds. Call the turn before the eighth, aloud, and say who has the bar. Two to the bar. Nine turns shuts the hill from the low streets.*

"*Went wrong: ran it straight and silent, and met the ninth. Also: two came to the bar, and nobody had said which two.*

"*Cost: a collarbone. A cellar in the Hythe, the night that nobody had said.*

"*Second try: called it, and said which two. Shut in nine turns.*

"*Why: because the road isn't straight. S.V.*"

Nobody spoke.

Kiva found that she had her right hand flat on the shelf, as she would have laid it on a pavement.

"It's a gate," she said. "The post. It isn't for practice. It's the handle of a gate." She could hear her own voice, and it sounded very young. "The drain turns there. I felt it turn. They put the lane on top of the drain so that everybody who ever trained in that yard would know the way to it in the dark. And they made you say it aloud. Which way, and who."

"*Call the turn before the eighth,*" said Vane.

He was looking at her.

"I stood in that yard on Saturday night and watched you make a carry team shout *post, left* over its shoulder. I thought that it was neat. I thought that you'd made it up." He put his hat down on the tin case. "You had. That's what I can't get past. Somebody wrote that down five hundred years ago, and a school lost it, and a committee voted on it, and you made it up again in a quarter of an hour because two litters hit a post."

"It was Perrin who said it was a lane call."

"I dare say it was." He turned to the Reader. "May that sheet go to the head of Stopgate? Today? I'll sign for it under the watch's name."

"It may go wherever the Line carries it. It's the Line's. I've only read it to you." She took off the second spectacles. "You'll want a spade, Officer. That plate shows the bar a hand above the ground. It's had a good many centuries of yard swept over its feet."

"I've a man with a saw," said Vane. "I told a sponsor so. I shall send him home and ask him whether he's a shovel."

---

Drum twenty-eight fell in at eighteen minutes to eight that evening, with thirty people at its rail to watch it.

Kiva was on the pavement. She had been told not to come nearer, and she did not need to. She saw it through the green door, past her aunt's shoulder.

The needle stood. It trembled. Then it went up, without any hurry, and came down. It had done it before anybody had drawn breath to say so.

Below it on the hill, behind two more green doors, two needles lay against their pins in a pair of drums that had been dry since Wednesday midnight.

The Chair's clerk had the paper. He broke the wax in front of Rusk and gave it to him.

Rusk read it aloud. He read it in a flat voice, to the wardens, and the fitters, and a pleasant man in a black coat who had taken off his hat.

"*If it is the drain: drum twenty-eight, on Monday the third of next month, with two dry drums between.*" He folded it along its crease. "It's the third. That's twenty-eight. Those are two dry drums." He looked down the street, at the dim brown windows of the middle stretch. "I've had six hundred families on boxes for five days, in the wet, to learn that I was wrong. I said that I'd carry that. It's heavier than it looked on Wednesday."

"A sixth time," said Mr. Adlam. He said it very quietly. "I shall have to write to the technical office that there is a thing which its patent does not describe. They will not like it. I rather think that I do."

"You stopped nothing," said Mara, to Rusk. "And you did no harm. I'd have done the same in your chair, and I said so in that room."

"You did. I'd it written." He took off his linen cuff guards, one after the other, which she had never seen him do, and put them in his pocket. "It's two days to twenty-nine, and two more to a school. Mrs. Rowan. You called the drum. You found the drain. I'm told that you've a sheet of paper, as of this morning, with a sluice on it." He held out the trunk plan, rolled. "It's yours. The hill, the bank, and the stopping of it. I'll sign whatever you draw, and I'll put my forty operators where you say."

Mara did not take it at once.

"I don't want it," she said.

"No. I'd noticed. That's a good part of why."

She took it. She held it for a moment in her left hand, the reading one, as she would have held a can that might be cut.

Then she looked round for her clerk.

"Kiva," she said. "The book. And find me a spade."

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-44.md

# Chapter 44 — Kiva: Two to the Bar

The man with the saw turned out to be a perfectly good shovel, and by nine on Tuesday he had the cross dug up.

Kiva watched it go. She stood in the Old Yard with the book against her chest, and a stranger in a leather apron put a spade into the mark that she had come down on since the summer and turned it over like a garden bed.

"Two hands down," he said. "There's iron."

It was a lid. It was a yard square, of the old thick sort, with a ring let into it, and it lay at the foot of the leaning post on the side toward the lane. It had been under the packed dirt for so long that the dirt had taken the print of the ring.

Three fitters lifted it with bars. A smell came up, cold and green.

"Lamp," said Mara.

She lay down at the edge of it, in her work apron, and let the lamp go on its chain. Kiva knelt beside her with the slate.

It was a square brick shaft, eight feet deep. At the bottom the Ferren main went by, black, a yard wide. The water in it rose a finger on the brick, and fell. And across the wall side of the shaft, back under the post, a foot above the water, hung a plate of iron as broad as a door, held in two grooves in the brick like a window in its sash.

"That's a penstock," said Mara. Her voice came up hollow. "That's as pretty a sluice gate as I ever saw. It's hanging on a screw." The lamp swung. "And the screw goes up. It goes up through the brick, on a slant. Kiva. Where does that slant come out?"

Kiva looked along it. She did not need to measure.

"The post," she said.

They all looked at the post.

It was black, and split, and bound with two iron bands. It stood a pace out from its row, and it leaned.

"It isn't a post," said the man in the apron. He had gone round to the wall side, and had his thumbnail in the split. "There's iron in the middle of it. It's a wooden coat on a spindle. And that's no split, mistress. That's cut. That's a socket, square, a hand across, to take a bar." He looked up at the bottom wall, thirty feet of brick a yard and a half behind him. "That's why it's out of the row. Put a bar through a post in that row and it'd strike the wall on the first quarter turn."

"And the lean?"

"It's on the rake of the screw. You'd not get a straight spindle down to a gate that's eight feet under and a pace back toward the wall." He patted the black wood, as a man pats a horse. "It was never crooked. It's aimed."

Somebody let out a breath behind them. It was Orla. She had been on her crate by the first shed since before the spade went in, with her stick between her boots.

She did not say anything. She got up, which took her some time, and came down the lane, and stood in front of the post as she might have stood in front of a person.

"One-and-twenty years," she said. "I've called you no help. In so many words. I said it as lately as the eighth month." The crooked face was doing something that Kiva had not seen it do. "I sat under a shut-off valve with my back to it, wondering what it was for, and told six hundred students that it wasn't in the manual."

"It wasn't," said Kiva.

"No. That's the worst of it. I was telling the truth." Orla laid the flat of her hand on the iron band. "I told Vane that I'd hold the lamp for his man. I'll hold it for a fitter. It comes to the same."

Mara had got up off her stomach. She stood at the edge of the shaft, wiping her left hand on her apron.

"I told a watch officer in the seventh month that every device in my house had a meter and a way to shut it down," she said. "I told my niece on Monday week that I'd found the meter for this, and not the other." She looked at the post, and at the hole where a cross had been. "A girl's been landing on it. Since the summer. I've been to that yard to watch her."

She began, rather helplessly, to laugh. Kiva had not heard her laugh like that since before the hall panel went dark.

"Well," said Mara, when she could. "*Two to the bar. Nine turns.* Let's see whether it remembers."

It did not remember.

They had a bar by ten, an ash pole as thick as a wrist, and four fitters on it, two to a side. They walked it round a quarter turn, and a half, and the screw groaned in the brick like a ship. At one full turn it stopped as though it had struck a wall.

"Rust," said the man in the apron. "And four hundred years of whatever the hill's washed into that slot. It wants oil, and it wants patience, and it wants somebody down that hole with a rake." He spat on his hands. "I can give you the oil."

Hollis Vane came at noon, as he had said that he would. He had the tin case under his arm. He looked at the open shaft, and at the bar standing through the post, and at four men in shirtsleeves leaning on it.

"I'm told that I owe a sponsor a lamp," he said.

"You owe me nothing," said Orla. "I'd no reason. I'd only a feeling about one. You said so."

"I did. And I gave it three days on the strength of a girl and a carry team. Somebody may write that down, since I shan't always be so sensible." He put the case down on her crate. "Rowan. What's it shut?"

Kiva had been waiting to be asked that. She had worked it out on the slate, with Mara, in the last hour.

"The hill from the street. Everything above this gate is the School's side. The drain that goes on along the lane, and the hill's rain, and one thing more." She pointed down the shaft. "There's a second mouth. A small one, in the north wall, above the gate. Mara had the lamp on it. It comes in from the core house."

"So that's how it would have reached your core," said Mara, to the keeper.

Ansty had come to look. She stood at the low wall with her keys.

"Round by the post," she said. "And I had a cork in my gully."

"If the gate's down, the breath stops here," said Kiva. "The core house never falls in. It can't; there's a door shut in the road. But it does nothing for the pumps. That's all below."

"And if it's shut, where does the hill's rain go?"

"Nowhere," said Mara. "It fills this yard to the top step in a wet night. So I shut it when I must, and not an hour sooner, and I open it when the bank's been seen to." She looked at the bar. "Supposing that it shuts at all."

"And if there's a thing in the street with an arm to put down?"

Mara did not answer at once.

"Every grating on Stopgate will be planked and bagged by tomorrow noon. Rusk's had carts out since six. The School's gully's done. That's Sorn's way, and it's a good one, and I'm taking it." She nodded at the shaft. "This is the one hole on the hill that I can't plank. I've a man going down it with a rake. It stays open until it's shut itself."

Vane looked at the hole for some time.

"Then I know where I'd go," he said, "if I were a thing that wanted water."

---

They filled twenty-six and twenty-seven on Tuesday at one, since there was no longer any reason for six hundred families to sit in the dusk.

Both drums went in before the fitter had finished counting sixty. Mara had her palm on the first. She took it away as one takes a hand off a stove.

"So that's my bank," she said. "Four-and-twenty fresh cans, and every one of them open for a slow sixty, in a house with the main going by under the floor. If the water's still, I've a chance. If it so much as rocks, I've filled them for nothing." She looked at her hand. "And every can in the Compact's store has had the hair in it since the thaw. They're halfway to listening before I've drawn the stopper. I want ash that keeps no time at all. A pinch, to salt each one. Something that's never heard a beat in its life."

"There's a shelf of it," said Kiva.

It was in the Wren House, at the end of the passage where the tins spent their night. Kiva had not been in that room since the spring. It ran from floor to ceiling, hundreds of small stoppered jars with a card round the neck of each, and Grader Halloran stood in front of it with her arms folded like a woman defending a larder.

"Plain," she said. "You want plain. So does everybody, this week. I've had the Halden technical office here, very civil, and I sent it away." She looked at Mara, and the work apron, and the license in its case. "You're the one who wrote *would mask trend* on a mast. I read the Works' circulars. What's it for?"

Mara told her. She did it in four sentences.

Halloran heard her out with her head a little on one side.

"Four-and-twenty," she said. "A pinch each. And you'll be back for thirty more, for your drums."
