"The drums can wait. They're on open links; each of them's alone. If the bank's taken out and the water's quiet, I can do a drum a day through the winter with whatever old stock the wardens have in their cupboards. I'm told there's a woman on Weighbridge Street who can smell a cut."

"Cobb. She can. She's better than my comb." Halloran turned to the shelf. She ran her eye along a row of brass labels, low down, where the cards were brown. "Handful, plain. I've three hundred and six years of it. Every one's a pinch from a husk that somebody killed and somebody tinned, and wrote the day on. I can't card what I can't keep. I said that in a hearing room in the seventh month."

"I was there."

"Then you know that I'm not a generous woman." She took down a jar. She read the card, and then she read it aloud, since nobody in that building seemed able to do anything without a witness. "*Sag Line. The 4th of the third month, 2511. Plain. Held 106.* That's a hundred and sixty-nine years on a shelf to be put in a pump." She set it on the table. "Four-and-twenty. I'll choose them. I'll write each one off by its number, with the reason, and you'll sign under me. And you'll not have the thirty. Not if the Chair asks. A shelf that gives whenever it's asked nicely isn't a shelf."

"That's fair," said Mara.

"It's not fair. It's what's left when I've done being frightened." Halloran took down another.

Three of Seven had come to carry. Jude was at the door, and Anwen, and Perrin with a crate.

"There's plain nearer than that," said Jude.

He had not said it loudly. He was looking at the cold-room door across the passage, with its three seals.

"I'm only saying it since somebody will. We've two drams on our own book that came off a husk on the Sag in the fourth month, before any of this got into the ash. Ward-trace. No hair. It's sat under wax for five months." He scratched his jaw. "That's two hundred pinches, by my reckoning. It'd do the bank and the drums and leave some over. And there's six hundred drams in that room of the loudest ash in Meridian, if anybody wanted a thing for a string to walk to that wasn't a drain."

"No," said Anwen.

She said it without heat. She had the green book under her arm, and she did not open it.

"The first isn't salt. I had it written that it isn't training ash, in the seventh month, in case anybody was tempted. It's the same answer. It's medical. It's for somebody's hand, on a night that we haven't had yet, and when that night comes I'd like there to be something in the tin." Her level eyes went to Jude. "You're on the line by name. You've a right to say it. I'm glad you did. I'd sooner it was said by one of us and refused than thought by all of us and left lying."

"And the second?"

"That's hers," said Anwen.

Kiva had known that it would come round to her. There was a bag in that room with her pencil on the tag.

"I wrote *Not for the ground,*" she said. "On two of them. I'd be the one saying that it was for the ground after all, since we'd found a use." She shook her head. "It's under three seals. One's the Works', and one's a street's, and Mrs. Cobb would walk up here in her overcoat and stand on the step. And I can't weigh what it would do. I'd be putting six hundred drams of a thing I don't understand on the cinders as bait for another thing I don't understand."

"It's evidence," said Halloran, without turning round. "I've a dram of it on this shelf, and a card that I've written four times and torn up. Nobody burns the only one of anything while I've the key."

"Then that's two things not spent," said Jude. He did not look put out. "I thought I'd ask. A man with a yield line gets to wondering what ash is for."

"It's for the next attempt," said Perrin. "That's what my dad says. He says it's the only thing it's ever bought anybody." He picked up the crate. "I'd like it known that I'm carrying three hundred years of history down a stair, and that if I trip, I'm going to keep walking and change my name."

---

Rhea Sorn sat in the Graywater watch room on Tuesday night with the map wall at her back, and ruled a line on a slate.

She had read that slate from the left for twenty-three years. *Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.* It was the whole of her trade in five words, and one could not buy a column with the one to its right. She had told a boy so in the sixth month, and he had written it down as though it were news.

There was nothing to the left of *Closed.* There never had been. A call began when a bell rang, and what one did about it went into the first column, and what it had cost went into the others afterward.

*I cut, and I count after, and I'm thought very honest for it.*

She had said that to a girl with her hand in a cloth. She had been rather pleased with it at the time, as one is pleased with a thing that is true and costs nothing.

"You've been looking at that for a quarter of an hour," said Pike.

He was mending a hem by the stove. He did it every Tuesday, whether a hem wanted it or not.

"I've been thinking about Sunday."

"I thought that you might be." He bit off the thread. "You said it was too dear."

"It is too dear. I've seen what two of those gaps did to her. I've seen Stroud eat off a spoon." Rhea put the chalk down. "She's seventeen. She's on my card. If she goes up over that hole and comes down with a hand that only grips, that's mine, whoever read what off a slate. I'm not sorry that I said no."

"I'd not ask you to be."

"Then what is it, Pike?"

He folded the canvas over his knee. He took his time.

"You told her you'd never named a price first. I was by. I thought, that's handsome, that's a thing she'll keep." He looked up. "On Sunday you named one. Good. Then you paid it for her, in front of forty people, before she'd opened her mouth. That's not naming a price. That's the same thing you've always done, the other way up. You cut, and she counts after."

Rhea did not answer.

It was a hard thing to hear from a man who had knelt at her hems for the best part of her time on the van. It was harder that he had said it while threading a needle.

*What's there?* she asked herself. It was her oldest question.

A catcher, with eight fingers that reported. A healer's card that said he would name the price to a foreman's face. A sponsor who had stood at a rail and said, *I don't know that it will work.* A girl who had said, in a yard, in front of the Chair, *Sorn's right that it isn't hers to have offered,* and had then asked a sensible question about a cage.

*And what's behind me?*

She turned round, in her chair, and looked.

The map wall. A lamp for every mast in the district, and nine of them lit, since she had a student sitting under each with a drum and a boy to run. A painted table with her company second on it and a remark in the last column. And a long way behind that, a culvert on the Ferren in the spring of '57, and a young blade who had gone in too far being hauled out of the wet by a Home Line woman who kept one hand shut on something at her throat.

Ilse Corran had not asked her whether she wanted pulling out. She had been glad of that for twenty-three years.

She had been a casualty. The girl was not a casualty. She kept putting her in that column, and it was the wrong one.

There was a stick on the stair. It came up slowly, a step at a time.

"They said I'd find you," said Orla Dane.

She was out of breath. She stood in the door of the watch room in her old coat, and looked at the map wall, and did not ask leave to sit.

"We've never much cared for one another," she said. "I'll save you the trouble of being civil. I've come about my candidate."

"She's my attached catcher."

"She's both. That's what I've climbed your stair to say." Orla lowered herself onto the bench. "I'm her sponsor. If a hand's the price of a thing that she does, I sign for the hand. I've done it once and had it countersigned. You're her officer. You sign for the floor, and for who's seated on it, and you've every right to say that it isn't a floor for her." The stick turned in the old hands. "Neither of us signs for her. I thought once that I could. I'd a boy of seventeen and a sum worked out to the last figure, and I never asked him anything, since I knew the answers."

"I know about your boy."

"Everybody does. It's why I'm believed." She said it quite flatly. "I'm not asking you to say yes. I'd not, in your place; I don't know that I shall in my own. I'm asking you not to say it's settled. If that thing finds the one hole on the hill, somebody will turn round and look at her whether you forbid it or not. I'd like her to have been left something to say."

"And her aunt?"

"Her aunt drew a plan with a gap in it. She wrote *I can't draw this part* in the margin, in ink, and showed it to Rusk like that, sooner than draw the child into it." Orla almost smiled. "She's licensed. It's a wonderful thing in a guardian."

Rhea looked at the slate.

She took up the chalk. She ruled a sixth column, at the left of the first, a narrow one. She wrote a word over it.

*Before.*

"That's new," said Pike.

"It's a Home Line notion. Vane's got one in a ledger. I thought that it was a clerk's fancy when he read it off in the Pit." She turned the slate to the stove. "If it moors, and if there's no other way, and if she says so herself without being looked at. Then I hear the price from the healer, to my face. I hear from her what she does if it's refused. And I want to know who has every piece of it that isn't hers. Then I sign, or I don't." She put the chalk down. "It isn't a yes."

"I didn't climb two flights for a yes," said Orla. "I'd have distrusted one."

---

Kiva went to the Wren House infirmary that evening, at the hour when the lamps were turned down, and found that she had no speech at all.

It was a long room with twelve beds, and Selka Dray had the one by the window. Her left leg lay outside the blanket in a board, from the hip to the heel, and she was reading by a shaded lamp with a pencil in her teeth.

She took the pencil out.

"You," she said.

"Me."

"If it's about my rule, I've written to the Chair already. I'll not unwrite it."

"It isn't. I want to ask you for something." Kiva stood at the foot of the bed. There was a chair, and she had not been invited to it. "I'll say all of it, and then what I do if you say no."

"You've got that off Pryce. Half the Circuit's doing it." The grey eyes were sunk, and perfectly sharp. "Go on."

"There's a thing that may come up in the street below the School. A string, or something like one. If it does, it'll put an arm down into the drain, and the arm will haul and ease on a count, as that cord did on your leg. If somebody's to take the arm off the water, they'll want the slack called. To the half count." She kept her hands still. "I can't call it. I tried on Sunday and I nipped a first-year's fingers. I've one hand that hears and one that doesn't, and I took the middle."

"I heard. Marten told me. He said she folded like a conjuror."

"Brask called it after that. He's good. But if it comes to the real thing, he's wanted on a bar, with Cade; there's a gate to be shut, and it's two still-doors' work." She drew a breath. "You had it across your leg for the best part of a quarter of an hour. You counted it. Eight to a breath, and it bites on the first four. You told me so lying on your back. I don't know anybody in Meridian who's been as near it for as long and kept the count."

Dray said nothing.

"I'm not seated for it. Sorn's said no, and it isn't to be asked of me, and nobody's asking. So this may be for nothing whatever. You'd be carried up a hill in a chair in the cold, to sit and watch planks." Kiva made herself finish. "And if you say no, it's Brask if he can be spared, and Wace if he can't. Wace has a good ear. He's never had it on him."

There was a long silence. Somewhere down the ward somebody coughed.

"You've not said that I owe you," said Selka Dray.

"You don't."

"You took a thing off my leg."

"That was mine to spend. You told me not to make a speech."

"So I did." Dray put the book down, open, on the blanket. Kiva saw that it was the Circuit's rules, and that the margins were full of pencil. "I'll tell you what I've been doing for seventeen days. I've been lying here with a blade arm and no leg, being of no use to anybody, and I find that I mind that a good deal more than I mind you. I'd not have thought it possible."

"Nor would I."

"Don't be pleased. It's not a compliment." Her jaw moved. "I'll do it. I've terms."

"Go on."

"There's a needle on you. A mast, in that yard, and Bexley at it with his flags. I'll not call a count for anything of yours that isn't being read. If the red goes up, I stop, in the middle of a word." She held up a second finger, exactly as Jude had once. "Marten carries the chair. With a porter. He'll not let anybody else, and I'll not have him argued with."

"That's two."

"The third is that it's written. That I objected to you twice, and that I think what I thought, and that I'm counting since I'm the one who can and for no other reason. I'd not have anybody suppose that I've come round."

"I'll write it tonight. I'll show you the page."

"You'll not. I'll take your word. I've noticed that it's good." Dray picked up her book again. "It bites on the first of the four. Not all four alike. The first is the worst, and then it only holds. I'd call *in* a hair before it lets go, since you'll want the time to move your hands. You'd have learned that if you'd lain under it."

"I'd not have liked to."

"No," said Selka Dray. "I didn't much."

Kiva went down the infirmary stair with her right hand on the rail.

She wrote it that night, on the right, where the reasons went. She put the three terms down as they had been said. Then she sat with the pencil for a while, and under them she wrote a thing that she had been not writing since Sunday.

*Sorn said no. I was relieved. Then I wasn't. I want it to be me. That isn't a reason; it's only what's so. If it comes, I'm to say what it costs, and what I do if it's refused, and who has the parts that aren't mine. I've three of those now. The cord. The count. The corner, after six.*

She looked at the last line. Brask had said yes to that one at the Hall on Monday, with his notebook open, as though she had asked him to hold a ladder.

*And if Brask is kept at the bar: Wace. Asked. He said, "I've been a post before."*

It did not look like being ready. It looked like a sheet with a great many corners, and somebody else's name on most of them.

---

Four of them signed the book on Wednesday at five, standing up, on the lid of the Low Lane's quench box.

It was a short entry. Anwen read it.

"*Wednesday the 5th. Three-door, K. Rowan: 0.59, from held. One go. Only on a foreman's direction, under the officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer. Cade keeps the word. If not directed, returned to held, whole.*" She looked up. "*Why:* I've left it bare. I'll fill it on the night, or I'll rule it through."

"That's a dram of mine that I didn't expect to see again," said Perrin. He signed. "I said *no* to this in the seventh month. I said *yes, with a brake* in the eighth. Tonight I've not the least idea what I think, and I'm signing so that there's something to say *off* to."

"Held, thirteen and fifty-three hundredths," said Kellan. He blotted it. "I've had the others' call drams out of the press. They're combed."

The fitters had the gate at seven turns by then.

Kiva had been to look. The bar stood through the black post, and four men in shirtsleeves sat on the ground beside it with their backs against the wall. Down the shaft, on a ladder, a boy with a rake was bringing up grey silt in a bucket.

"It's the slot," said the man in the apron. "The last two turns, the gate's to go down into its groove in the floor of the drain, and the groove's full to the brim. He's been at it since noon. I'd say midnight. I'd not say it loudly."

Drum twenty-nine fell in at twenty to eight.

It went as the others had. There was a crowd at its rail, and a needle that stood, and trembled, and went up without any hurry at all. Nobody cheered and nobody cried out. They had seen it before. A warden rang her handbell once, and people on the top stretch of Stopgate came to their doors and stood there with their coats on.

"Friday for the core house," said Mara. "By the float. I shall have my gate down by then if I've to go down that ladder with a spoon." She had the plan under her arm, and forty names on a list. "And the bank on Thursday night at one, when the sump's lowest. Every operator that Rusk has, and eleven students who stood a Bench. I've told them to sleep tomorrow afternoon. I don't suppose that one of them will."

The planks were down. Kiva had walked the hill at dusk to see them. Every grating between the tram rails had three thick boards across it and a row of sandbags on the boards, forty of them, from the Brickfields road to the black gate. It looked like a street that was expecting a flood.

She was in the bottom yard at eleven, on no duty at all; she had gone to see whether the boy with the rake had come up.

He had not. There was a lamp down the shaft still, and the scrape of iron on brick.

She was standing at the gap in the low wall, with her hand on the cold stone, when she felt it change.

It did not grow. She would have said, afterward, that it turned over. The slow even push that she had had under her palm for nine days had been a thing going by. It was not going by any longer. It had stopped, somewhere below her on the hill, and was standing still, and getting deeper, as water deepens behind a hand.

"Noa," she said. And then, as loudly as she had ever said anything, up at the black bulk of the core house roof: "*Noa! What's the drum doing?*"

There was a pause of perhaps two counts.

The School's bell rang.

It rang two and two and two, over her head, and it did not stop. And through it came a thin cracked voice that she would have known anywhere.

"*It's the drum! It's the drum, it isn't the mark! It's climbing! It's the drum!*"

She ran to the wicket.

She had no business there. She was recovery, and dark, and seventeen. But the wicket stood open, and there was nobody in it, and she looked down Stopgate.

It was a long straight hill, wet, with a lamp at every twentieth pace. Near the top of it, not forty paces below her, the lamps had a different color to light.

It lay in the crown of the road. It was between the tram rails, where the planks were, and it ran from the rail of the twenty-eighth drum house up to the rail of the twenty-ninth, sixty paces and more. It was thin. It was blue.

It was not a stroke standing in the air, like the gap at the edge of a door. It was lying down. It was a seam, a hand wide, in the surface of the street, as though somebody had drawn a knife along a tablecloth and the color of the table were showing through.

The planks over the nearest grating had gone white. She saw frost run along them, and round the sandbags, like spilled milk.

Then, the whole length of that seam, things began to come up.

They did not come one above another. She had seen a string climb out of a hatch a knot at a time. These rose together. Eight grey shoulders came up through the blue, side by side, eight paces apart, each on a pair of legs as thick as lampposts, and between each and the next there ran a white cord at the height of a man's chest.

It was a string. It was a string laid on its side.

There was not a top to it. Every one of the eight had a blunt head of its own, and every head was turning, slowly, a quarter of a circle, as a weathercock turns.

Below her, on the hill, eleven handbells went off like a dropped tray.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-45.md

# Chapter 45 — Kiva: Paid Out

Mara came up the ladder out of the sluice shaft with silt to the elbows, and she had heard the bell.

"Is it the mark?"

"It's the drum. Noa says so. I've seen it." Kiva had run back across the cobbles. She was out of breath, and her knee was telling her about it. "It's in the road. Between twenty-eight and twenty-nine. It's a tear, lying down, and there's a string in it laid on its side. Eight. Every one of them's got a head."

Mara stood quite still, with her hands held away from her apron.

"It's a night early," she said. "I've a bank to take out, and all of my forty but the night shift are in their beds." She looked down the shaft, where a lamp still burned and a rake still scraped. "And I've a gate that wants two turns."

"The fitters have the bar."

"The fitters have had the bar since Tuesday." She wiped her left hand, the reading one, on the only clean corner of her apron, with great care. "Listen to me, since I shan't be here. That gate's to come down the moment the slot's clear, and if four men can't shift it, it's two still-doors. Cade and Brask. You know that. You know who has what better than anybody on this hill; you've been writing it down for a week. Say it aloud, to whoever's standing nearest. Don't suppose that they know."

"Where will you be?"

"The pump house. There's a Works van at the foot of the hill, and Rusk has had a dozen of the night shift on that floor since the bank fell in. If I'm to quench four-and-twenty cells tonight, I must find the other dozen, and I've a list in my pocket of where they sleep." She was already unpinning the apron. "I'll be on the municipal wire. Mrs. Ansty has a key in her lodge and a clerk who can work it."

She stopped, with the apron in her hands.

"I'll say one thing more, and I'll not say it twice. I shan't be on this hill. I'm glad of it, and I'm ashamed of being glad." Her voice was quite level. "Whatever's asked of you tonight, or not asked, I shan't be at a rail with my hand on my coat. It won't be me who looks at you. I told you in the spring that I wanted you safe without owning what you chose. I find that I can only manage it from two miles off."

"Mara—"

"Don't. I've a bank." She put the apron into Kiva's arms, as she had once put a book there. "Mind that boy on the ladder. He's fourteen, and he thinks that a bell is for other people."

She went out by the wicket, walking fast.

---

Hollis Vane had the hill by ten minutes past eleven, and he took it as he took everything, at a walk.

"This is a response," he said. He stood in the black gateway with his slate, and his outdoor voice went down Stopgate and across the yard behind him. "It's mine. Wardens, your bells, and your red handles; I want every box on this hill dark before its door's shut. Bottom forty, down. Top two-and-twenty, up, by the lane. Carry, you've walked it. You know where the post is."

"*Post, left!*" said somebody on the hill, who had not been asked. "*Keep right!*"

"There," said Vane. "It's got about."

The litters began to come.

It was not Saturday. Kiva knew that in the first minute. On Saturday there had been laughter on the hill, and a child who had not been told to be quiet. Tonight there was only the sound of feet, and doors, and a handbell going steadily along the top stretch like a pulse.

She was at the gap in the low wall. It was her place, and she had been put there twice. Pru stood beside her with the shuttered lamp.

"Through here. Lane's ahead. *Post, left, keep right.* Say it back."

"Post left, keep right."

They said it back. Every team said it. Pettit came through at a trot with a real woman on his poles, a very old one, wrapped to the chin, and he bawled it over his shoulder with the fervor of a convert. The call ran ahead of him down the dark lane, and came back from the sheds in Healer Aske's dry voice, where the Wren House cart stood at the side gate with its tailboard down.

Nobody struck the post.

There was a bar through it. Four fitters stood at the bar in their shirtsleeves, not turning it, since there was a boy down the shaft at their feet and one does not turn a screw over a boy. They had moved the litter road a yard to the north with a strip of white tape.

"How long?" said Kiva, to the man in the apron.

"He's got the east groove clear. He's on the west." The man did not take his eyes from the hole. "A quarter of an hour. I said midnight. I'd be glad to be made a liar."

A van came up the hill.

She heard it before she saw it, the whine of its rings on the wet setts. It stopped below the twenty-eighth drum house, where the blue began. Slate blue coats got down from it, and a square shape with a roll of canvas on each shoulder, and a tall woman with a white streak in her hair, who stood for a moment in the road with her hands in her pockets, reading.

"Rowan," said Vane. He had not turned. "You read ground. You've a first-year who can stand at a gap and say four words. Give her the gap. Come to this gate and tell me what that thing does at the top. I can't see the top and the bottom both."

"Pru," said Kiva. "The gap. Yours. All of it."

"Mine," said Pru. Her voice hardly shook.

Kiva went to the gate.

---

From the black gateway one could see the whole of it.

It stood in the crown of the road, between the rails, eight grey shapes abreast down sixty paces of hill. Each was the size of a Flask's knot, as big as a barrel, on a pair of thick legs. The white cord ran from shoulder to shoulder, and from the cord a wall of frost hung down to the setts and stood above it, thin as breath on a window. It cut Stopgate in two the long way.

Every head was turning. They did not turn together. That was what made her skin creep. Eight blunt tops went round a quarter, and back, each in its own time, like eight people in a dark room listening for the same sound.

"Count them from the top," said Vane. "The one nearest us is the first."

"The first's at the rail of twenty-nine. The eighth's at twenty-eight. They're eight paces apart." She had her book on her forearm. "They're seeking. They've not found it. The planks are white, under every one, and nothing's gone down."

"Sorn's way. Good." He raised his voice. "*Sorn! It's a string on a street! It's yours!*"

Down the hill four wards had brought a gate up, twelve paces below the eighth knot, as bright as a shopfront.

Eight heads went past it.

"I didn't suppose that they'd care for it," said Rhea Sorn. Her voice came up the hill quite clearly. She had not raised it. "Leave it lit. It's the rule, and it'll do for the husks. Pike. Your cloth, under the eighth. I'm taking it from the bottom."

Kiva watched her do it.

She had seen that cut before, at a weir, at first light. Rhea Sorn had no Stride. She did not go up the back of anything. She walked to the lowest of the eight as a woman walks to a door, and stopped at an arm's length, and waited, with her blade arm down.

"*Not yet,*" said Pike, from the hem. "It's turning. *Now.*"

The light was yellow, and level, and it lasted most of a second.

The eighth knot fell in through its own shoulders. Sixty drams came down on the canvas like sand out of a sack, and lay.

"*Dark! One.*"

The cord came off it. It whipped back up the hill, the loose end of it, and the seventh knot took it in at the shoulder as a reel takes a line. There was no gap. There were seven, with a cord from each to the next, and a foot of blue seam lying open in the road where the eighth had been.

"Sealed," said Pike. "Fifty-two." He had his palm on the neck. "It's loud. It's as loud as anything I had off the Pit. Red."

"Then that's how it's done," said Rhea. "It's slow. I've done slow. The seventh."

"Sir," said Kiva.

She had not been watching the bottom. She had been told to watch the top.

At the rail of the twenty-ninth drum house, eight paces above the first knot, the blue line had moved. It had run on up the hill, in the crown of the road, as a crack runs in ice. And at the end of it something was coming up through the setts. It had shoulders.

"There's another," she said. "At the top. It came up as she cut. There's eight again."

Vane turned his head.

He looked for a long moment. Down the hill Rhea Sorn was walking to the seventh knot. Pike had his second cloth down.

"*Now.*"

The yellow light. The sound of sand.

And at the top, the blue ran on another eight paces, toward the wall, and another pair of shoulders rose out of the road.

"It's not growing," said a voice at Kiva's elbow. "It's being paid out."

It was Lowen Brask. He had a litter pole in one hand; he had just come up from the lane. His square face was the color of the wall.

"That's a hold," he said. "I said so in the Pit and I'll say it here. There's a tear, and it's kept open, and what's in it comes through for as long as it's kept. Only there's nobody keeping it. There's no square." He looked down at the wet setts between his boots. "It's the drain. The whole main's hauling and easing under that seam, on a four and a four. I stood a summer doing that with my shoulders. The road's doing it by itself."

"So she can cut at the bottom until morning," said Vane.

"And it'll walk to the top while she does. It'd come up without her, at the drain's own pace, until it ran out of road. Every cut only lets the next one out at once. She's bailing, and she's hurrying it up your hill."

Vane wrote on his slate. He wrote very little.

"Bexley's on the roof. There's a clerk on the key in the lodge." He tore the corner off a sheet. "*To M. Rowan, the Ferren pump house. It is a held tear. The main is holding it. Your bank tonight, as soon as you have the hands, and the pumps stopped. The holes are planked; it has no arm down. H.V.*" He gave it to a runner. "That's the Works' part, and I'm glad of it. Sorn! Hold your cut! You're feeding it!"

Rhea had stopped already. She stood below the sixth knot with her blade dark, looking up the hill, and Kiva saw her count.

"Eight," she said. "I've cut two and there are eight." She put her hands back in her pockets. "Then I'm a gate and a pair of eyes until somebody stops that water. I don't like it. I've not liked anything since the thaw."

The ninth knot's head had begun to turn.

It did not turn toward the gate. It turned east, slowly, a quarter of a circle, and stopped. It was pointing at a house.

It was a tall house, near the top, with brass on the door. There was a light behind the fanlight, small and steady and yellow.

"That's a box," said Kiva. "Somebody's not pulled their handle."

---

Anwen Pryce had been given the end of the wall again, and this time the wall was walking.

That was Mace's notion. He had stood behind his line at the foot of the blue for one slow minute, looking at boots, and then he had said, in the voice in which he asked for the salt, that a gate was no use to a thing that would not look at it. What was wanted was a porch.

"Every house on this hill has a box in it," he had said. "A box is a lamp. Until the handle's pulled, it's the brightest thing that string can read, and it's ten paces from a front door with people behind it. So we stand between. A wall along the curb, from the bottom to the top, and it goes up the hill as the wardens do. Ten of you won't cover sixty paces. You'll cover the house that's lit."

Ten of them made forty paces of silver, cart-wide each, along the east curb. Six were the night van's. Four were students. Anwen had the uphill end, with Tibb on her left hand and the night on her right.

*If it dips, I'll hear it.*

She had learned an end in a culvert, standing still. She had not learned one that moved. A wall that walked had to be let go at the back and brought up at the front, a holder at a time, each coming up inside the next as a relief comes. It was Graywater's oldest trick. It was only that nobody did it for a hundred paces up a hill.

"Fifty-four's dark," said a warden's voice, behind her. "Fifty-six, there's nobody in. Dark."

"Step," said Anwen. "Tibb. I'm coming off. Now."

She let go of nothing. That was the craft of it. She leaned on Tibb's wall until hers held no weight at all, and then it was not there, and she had gone three paces up the wet pavement and brought it up again, new, at the top.

Six paces to her right, across the gutter, a frost wall stood twice her height. She could feel the cold of it on her cheek. Above it the cord ran white from one grey shoulder to the next, drawing up and letting down.

She did not look at it. She looked at the curb.

"Fifty-eight," said the warden. Her voice changed. "Fifty-eight's lit. Mr. Garland. *Mr. Garland!* Your handle!"

There was a light behind the fanlight. Anwen saw it at the edge of her eye, small and yellow.

There was a head turning, too. It was the ninth, the new one, eight paces up the hill from where the wall ended. It came round east and stopped.

"That's on us," said Tibb. He had stopped humming. "Pryce. That's yours. It's past the end."

"I know where it is."

It was eight paces beyond her. There was nobody to bring a wall up inside. There was only the pavement, and a tall house with brass on the door, and a grey thing in the road that had found the one lamp on the hill.

The cord swung.

It did not come fast. The ninth knot leaned, as the Flask had leaned at the Pit, and the white line between it and the next below bellied out toward the east curb like a rope with a weight hung on it. The frost came with it. It crossed the gutter.

"*Step!*" said Anwen. "All of it! Eight paces! I'm going long!"

She went.

She let go of Tibb's wall without waiting to feel it take, which was a thing that she had been told for four weeks never to do. She ran eight paces up the wet stone and stood in front of a green door with her back to the brass, and put up her hands.

The wall came. It was hers alone, a cart wide, with nothing on its left and nothing on its right.

The frost met it.

It did not strike. It leaned. It was the weight of a string's knot, sixty drams with legs under it, and it came onto her two hands as a full tin comes onto a shelf. She had held that weight before, with three beside her, for forty minutes. She had never held it with nobody.

*It'll bear weight.* She had said that about a different thing. She found that it was true of this one.

"I've an end with no middle," she said. She said it in a carrying voice, level, as one reports a tin. "Fifty-eight. I'm holding. I want a neighbor on my left and I want that handle pulled. In that order."

"Coming up," said Tibb. Then, sharply: "Stiles. You're short. You're a hand short on the outside, and she's got nothing to lean on if you join like that."

"So I am," said Stiles.

She felt them come. It was like hearing a cart turn into the street. Tibb's wall came up inside her left edge, and beyond his, Stiles's, snug this time, and the weight on her two hands became a weight on six. She did not dip. She knew that she had not, though there was nobody to tell her.

The green door opened behind her.

"You're being rescued," said a voice that she had known since it was a first-year's with purple hands. "It's on a count. There's a lady holding your porch up, so we're to be quick."

"I'll not leave Admiral," said an old man.

"He's coming. He's got his own handle. Brask, the box."

There was a sound from inside the house, a flat iron *clack.* The light in the fanlight went out.

Anwen felt the weight go off her hands as one feels a hand lifted from a shoulder. The ninth head turned away. It went round west, and north, and stopped pointing at nothing, and the cord drew back across the gutter to the crown of the road.

"Dark," said the warden. "Fifty-eight. And about time."

They came out past her. Perrin had the chair in his arms, with an old man in it and a cage on the old man's knee. Lowen Brask walked behind with a hand on the back rail. A bird was saying something disagreeable.

"I'd like it known," said Perrin, as he went by, without turning his head, "that I mind every one of these. That's Pryce. She's held a string on her own outside a front door, and I've had my back to it. Again."

"Walk," said Anwen.

She did not laugh. She found afterward that she had wanted to.

Mace was behind her. She had not heard him come.

He was looking at her boots. He looked at them for some time. Then he looked along the wall, at the place eight paces down the hill where she had let go of a join without waiting for it.

"You came off Tibb before he had you," he said.

"Yes."

"I've a card in a drawer that says you never do that."

"I know what it says."

"Why did you?"

She had the answer. It was not a tidy one.

"Because there was a door with people behind it, and the end of the wall was in the wrong place. I'd have been right about the join and they'd have had a hem through the fanlight." She kept her eyes on the curb. "I said so as I went. *All of it. Eight paces. I'm going long.* If I'd gone silent, I'd have been wrong."

"Hm," said Mace.

He wrote on his board. He did not show her.

"Step," he said. "It's not done. There's an eleventh coming up, and the sixties are lit."

She stepped.

---

The head of it reached the wall at half past eleven.

Kiva had watched it come. Nobody had cut it again, and it had come all the same, as Brask had said that it would, only slower. She had called every one to Vane as it stood up, in her carrying voice, with the number. *The tenth, sixteen paces above twenty-nine. The eleventh, at the last grating. The planks are holding.* She had written the minute beside each.

The blue line ran up the crown of Stopgate like a crack in a plate. It came to the stone sill of the black gate, and there it stopped.

It did not come through. She saw that. Outside the gate the main lay shallow, a yard under the setts, with a grating over it every twenty paces. Inside, it dived. It went down eight feet to a brick barrel, and the blue would not follow it there. It ended at the sill, clean, as a crack ends at the edge of a tile.

The twelfth knot came up in the gateway.

She had gone back from it already. Vane had her by the sleeve, and they stood on the cobbles, ten paces inside, and looked at a grey thing as big as a barrel standing under the School's device with frost running up the oak on either hand.

"The twelfth," she said. Her voice was not steady. "In the gate. That's all the road it's got."

"And it's taken mine," said Vane. He turned his head, and his outdoor voice went over the wall and down the hill. "*Wardens! The gate's shut to you! Whatever's left of the top comes down, by the curb, behind the porch, with its boxes dark!*"

A handbell answered him from the street, twice.

"Keeper!" said Vane.

"I've seen it," said Ansty.

She was at the iron door of the core house. She had not hurried. She had the pin out of a red lever already, and she held it up in two fingers so that the watch officer could see it had been drawn.

"That's a thing that reads," she said. "And I've the biggest lamp on the hill behind this door. I said that I'd pull when it was at my wall. It's at my wall."

She pulled.

There was a sound from inside the square brick house, deep and rattling, felt through the cobbles more than heard. It was a weight falling a long way, on purpose.

And the hum stopped.

Kiva had lived inside that hum for six months. It had come through the Low Lane's wall all day, like a kettle thinking about boiling, and she had long ago ceased to hear it. She heard it stop. It was like a held breath let go. All over the upper school, the windows that had been dim went brown.

"Rods down," said Ansty. She put the pin in her pocket. "Core's safe. The School's on its boxes, and I'll have every one of those pulled inside ten minutes. There's nothing in my house now for it to want."

The twelfth head was turning.

It went past the core house. It went past the Low Lane, and the gully with its planks and sandbags. It came round west, along the foot of the bottom wall, toward a gap in a low wall and a strip of packed dirt beyond it.

It stopped.

*I know where I'd go,* a watch officer had said, on Tuesday, *if I were a thing that wanted water.*

"It's the shaft," said Kiva.

She was already running.

"*The lid!*" She had never shouted so loudly in her life. "*Get the lid on! It's found the hole! Is that boy up?*"

He was not up.

She came through the gap with her knee screaming. Pru was there, flat against the wall, with the lamp. The four fitters were at the bar. And down the square brick shaft, at the foot of the leaning post, a lamp still burned, and a rake still scraped on iron.

"*Ned!*" The man in the apron was on his knees at the edge. "Up! Leave the rake! Up the ladder, now!"

"There's a hand's breadth left in the west groove—"

"*Now!*"

Something was coming along the wall.

It came from the gateway, at the height of a man's chest. It was white, and as thick as a wrist, and it did not hang from anything. It reached. It came along the foot of the brick as a hawser is paid out along a quay, drawing up and letting down, and where it passed, the wall went grey with frost.

It was an arm, three-and-twenty paces long, and it was feeling for a hole.

"Cade!" said Kiva.

She did not know where he was. She said it as one says a role on a floor.

"*Carry! The shaft! There's a boy on a ladder!*"

He came out of the dark of the lane like a dray.

He had been bringing an empty litter back for a next house that was by now going down the hill instead. He let go of the poles. He did not ask what, or where. He went down on his chest at the edge of the shaft, with his legs across the white tape, and put one long arm into the hole.

"You're being rescued," said Perrin, into the dark. "Don't argue. I've had an old man argue. Take my wrist. Not the ladder. *Me.*"

There was a scrabble. A small hand came up into the lamplight and shut on his forearm.

He lifted.

He did not haul. He stood up. He got one knee under him, and then the other foot, and he rose off the packed dirt with a boy of fourteen hanging from one arm like a bucket from a crane, and swung him clear, and set him down against the shed wall four paces off.

"There," he said. "That's one that I saw."

"The lid," said the man in the apron. "Bars! Get it over!"

Three fitters had the iron by its ring. It was a yard square, and it had taken three of them to lift it out on Tuesday with daylight and all morning. They dragged it. It came across the dirt a hand at a time, grating.

The arm came through the gap.

Kiva was six paces from it. She had her back against the eighth post, the last of the north row, and she could not have moved.

It did not hurry. It went by her at chest height, humming a little, and the cold came off it like the cold off a cellar door. It found the leaning post. It went round the foot of it, on the lane side, and tipped, as water tips over a sill.

The lid was half across.

The arm went down beside it. It went down through the gap that was left, a foot wide, into eight feet of brick shaft, and she heard it meet the water.

It was a small sound. It was the sound of a rope's end dropped into a well.

For a moment nothing happened.

Then the cord drew up, a hand. All along the foot of the bottom wall, and through the black gate, and down seventy paces of wet hill, ten grey shapes leaned back a little, together. And eased.

In. And out.

"It's in the drain," said the man in the apron. He had let go of the ring. "It's got its arm down my slot."

Kiva put her right palm flat against the post behind her.

She could feel it. She did not need the ground. It came up through the old wood, slow and even and many, as it had come up through a doorpost on the Brickfields road. It was stronger than it had been. It was a great deal stronger.

It was not going by any longer. It was being driven.

"Vane," she said.

He was in the gap. He had seen it go down.

"Say it."

"It's pumping. The main. It's the same as the trough." She took her hand off the wood. "If my aunt stops her pumps now, the water won't lie still. She can't fill. And if Sorn cuts, it runs down that arm into the drain, and it's under the pump house in an hour."

"And the gate?"

"It can't come down. There's a thing in the slot."

Vane did not write that. He stood and looked at a white cord going down into the ground at the foot of a post.

The runner came through the gap behind him. It was a School porter, with a slip of paper off the key in the keeper's lodge, and he was holding it out in front of him as though it were hot.

Vane read it aloud. He read everything aloud.

"*Pump house, eighteen minutes to twelve. I have nineteen hands of twenty-four and the rest coming. Pumps stopped two minutes, on trial. The water is still moving. It moved less at twenty past eleven, when I came in and chalked it. Something is in it. Pumps running again. I cannot fill over this. Tell me when it is out. M. Rowan.*"

He folded the slip.

"*Tell me when it is out,*" he said.

The yard was very quiet. There were perhaps twenty people in it now. The fitters stood at their bar. Lowen Brask had come to the gap, and Rhea Sorn behind him, with her coat open and her hands out of her pockets. Orla was on her crate by the first shed, where she had been since the bell. A tall grey figure stood at the side gate, by the Wren House cart, and beside her a man with a pin in his lapel.

Somebody was setting up a tripod under the shed lamp. It was Noa. He had come down off the roof with the travelling mast on his back, and nobody had told him to.

Kiva looked at the cord.

It lay in the angle of the post, where it left the ground, drawing up and letting down. It had a slack in it. Four counts of haul, and four of ease. She knew what it weighed on the slack. She had held one like it in a tray between her palms.

She knew what was four paces from it. She had not looked yet. She would have to put a door down and find out.

Nobody in the yard looked at her.

She saw them not do it. She saw a watch officer study his slate, and a Torch study the wall. She saw the Placement Chair fold her hands. They had every one of them heard it said on a blue brick floor, and they were keeping to it as people keep to the edge of a pavement.

*It is not to be asked of her.*

It was the kindest thing that anybody had ever written about her. She understood, standing there with her back to a post, that it had a second half which nobody had been able to write. If she was not to be asked, then nobody could say it for her. Not her sponsor, and not her officer, and not an aunt two miles away with nineteen hands.

It would have to be said by the one person in the yard who would rather have been told.

Her mouth was dry. She had an answer, and for once it was not standing ready. She had to go and fetch it, a piece at a time.

"I'd like to say what it costs," said Kiva.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-46.md

# Chapter 46 — Kiva: Call the Turn

"Then say it," said Hollis Vane.

He had not looked surprised. He had his chalk in his hand already.

"I'd like it in the order that Sorn asked for." Kiva kept her back against the post. It was something to stand against. "What it is. What it costs. What I do if it's refused. And who has every piece of it that isn't mine."

Rhea Sorn's head came round.

"Who told you that I'd asked for an order?"

"Orla. On the tram. She said that it wasn't a yes."

"It wasn't." The pale eyes rested on her. "Go on. I'm listening for the last part hardest."

"That arm's a hem under load. It's got a slack; four hauling and four easing. I stand at the hole. I put a ward under it for one tap, on the slack, and it comes off the water. It throws me. I fold, and I go with the throw four paces, and I put it down in dry ground and stand on it. Hand, Ember, still." She heard her own voice, thin in the cold yard. "Then it's hauling on me and not on the drain. The slot's clear. The gate can come down behind it. And there's nothing driving the main, so my aunt can stop her pumps and have the water lie."

"For how long?" said Vane.

"I held a corner for ten in the Pit. That was three of them. This is ten, on a longer arm. I'll not hold it. That's the first piece that isn't mine." She drew a breath. "I plant it. After six, it goes to somebody who can stand under it, and I shut. That was to be Brask."

"It still is," said Lowen Brask, from the gap.

"Not if the bar wants you. Four fitters have had that gate since Tuesday, and it's stuck at seven. If the last two turns are still-doors' work, that's you and Cade. *Two to the bar.* Then the corner's Wace's, with Moss at his other end. A plank. I asked them on Monday." She made herself say it slowly, as she would have called a mark. "I'm saying it now, before, so that if it turns, it's a turn that's been called."

"The count," said Rhea.

"Not mine. I nipped a first-year on Sunday. Brask's, if he's free. If he's at the bar—"

"Then it's mine," said a voice from the lane.

They had come in by the side gate. Kiva had not heard them for the bell. There was a thin boy in Wren House green, and a porter, and between them a wooden chair with a rail, such as the infirmary used on its stairs. A red head was in it. A leg in a board stuck out in front like a bowsprit.

"I heard that bell from my bed," said Selka Dray. She was the color of tallow, and she had a blanket round her shoulders. "I said that I'd come. I'd terms. There's a needle on her; I can see Bexley's tripod. Put me down by that post, Marten, where I can see the hole. Not so near. I've been near."

They put her down beside Kiva, against the eighth post, the last of the north row, five paces from the shaft.

"The cord on Seven's rung is Finch's, on her own cloth," said Kiva. "The wax is Pryce's. The word's Cade's; he may say *off* from the bar, and Orla may, and I may, in two places." She had come to the end of it. It was a long list. There was hardly anything left on it with her name. "That's who has what. I've only the middle."

"The price," said Rhea. "I'll hear that from him."

Healer Aske had walked down the lane. He had no coat on. He took her left hand without a word and turned it to Pru's lamp, and pressed the palm, and the wrist, and looked at the nails.

"No white," he said. "I looked for it. I'd have been glad of it." He did not let go. "I'll say this to the foreman's face and to the officer's, since that's what I wrote on a card. I don't know. The two fingers paid the last time, since they'd been hurt first. They've nothing left. So it comes out of the next thing, and the next thing's the hand. I think it goes to the elbow."

"And comes back?" said Vane.

"I think so. I've no case to think it from. Nobody has done this three times and let a healer watch." His dry voice did not rise. "It may be that she keeps a hand that grips and doesn't feel. Like her aunt's right, only the whole of it. That's the price as near as I can say it, and I'll not round it down to be obliging."

Nobody spoke. Along the foot of the wall the white cord drew up, and let down.

"And if I say no?" said Rhea.

"Then you cut from the bottom, and it's paid out at the top, and it stands on this hill with its arm in the main." Kiva had that ready. It was not a good answer, and she had made it on purpose. "The pumps go on running in step. My aunt can't fill over moving water. It's as it was this morning, with a string added, until a cell fouls or somebody thinks of a thing that I haven't. I'd not blame you. I was glad on Sunday when you said it."

"Were you?"

"For about an hour."

Rhea took a slate from inside her coat. It was not Vane's. It had six columns on it, and the sixth was narrow, and ruled at the left.

"Did anybody look at you?" she said. "Just now. Before you spoke."

"No. I watched you all not."

"Hm." The chalk moved. She read it out as she wrote it, which Kiva had never heard her do. "*Before: the left hand, to the elbow. Her healer does not know that it returns. Offered. Not asked.*" She looked up. "Under my hand, then. I've said for twenty-three years that I count after. I find that I've a column for the other."

"I sign for the hand," said Orla.

She had come down from her crate. She stood with both palms on the head of her stick.

"As her sponsor. I've seen it clean twice. I've seen it missed three times. It isn't on her paper and I'll not put it there." She did not look at Kiva. She looked at the cord. "And I don't know that it will work. I said that at the Pit and I was ashamed of how new it felt. It's a little less new."

"I am a witness," said Ysra Crest, from the shed. "I have a page with one line on it. Mr. Bexley has two flags."

"I've the pen on her," said Noa. His voice cracked. "I've had it on her since she put her hand to that post."

Vane wrote.

He wrote for longer than he had in the Pit. He held the slate up to the lamp.

"*Direction to K. Rowan, catcher, T7, attached, under R. Sorn's hand. Three doors in sequence, hand, Ember, still, declared. Cost, as stated before by her healer: the left hand, to the elbow; he does not know that it returns. Reason: an arm of a string of ten in the Stopgate sluice, driving the Ferren main, and a bank that cannot be filled while it drives. No other hand on this hill takes it off the water. Offered by the candidate. Not asked. The count is S. Dray's. The corner after six is L. Brask's, or Wace's.*" He lowered it. "Do you take it?"

There was a thing to be done first. It was in a green book, and the book was not in the yard.

"Pryce reads the why," said Kiva. "Before each one. It's her condition. She's on the wall."

"Then somebody fetch her," said Rhea. "Mace has Stroud."

It was three minutes. Anwen came through the gap with frost on one sleeve, and the book under her arm, and her face shut. She did not ask what had been said. She looked at the cord, and the half-covered hole, and the hand in the healer's two.

She wrote standing, against Perrin's back. It was two lines.

"*Wednesday the 5th. Three-door. For: to take an arm off the Ferren main, so that a gate can be shut and a bank filled. Likely cost: her left hand, to the elbow, and perhaps kept.*" She shut the book on her finger. "That's what's written. Is it still what's so?"

"Yes."

"Then I've nothing to add." Her level eyes came up. "Ask me."

"Pryce," said Kiva. Her throat had closed. "The wall. For whatever comes up after."

"Mine," said Anwen, and went.

"I'm frightened," said Kiva. "I'd like that written."

"It is," said Vane. "I'd begun the word before you said it."

"Then yes."

---

It took four minutes to set, and every piece of it was said aloud.

"Ten blades," said Vane. "One to a knot, from outside, dark. You light after the foot. You cut on one word, and the word's hers. Sorn has the twelfth, in my gate, since that's the one with the arm. Renn the eleventh. Talley the tenth. The rest as the Torch sets you."

"The word's *back,*" said Kiva. "Not before the gate's down, and not before my aunt says that the water's lying. On the slack."

"Ten cloths," said Pike. "Over the planks, where there are planks. Finch has hers under the eleventh knot."

"The wire. Bexley can't work a key and a pen." Vane looked round. "Three words, to the pump house, as they're said. *Planted.* On that, the Works stops its pumps and quenches its bank; a dead cell can't be cooked. *Down,* when the gate's seated. *Back.* I want a runner at the lodge door who can shout."

"I can shout," said the boy called Ned.

He had got up from the shed wall. He had silt to the hair.

"I'm told that you think a bell's for other people," said Vane. "You'll do. Go."

The fitters had the bar. They stood two a side, in their shirtsleeves, with the ash pole lying fore and aft along the wall like a boat's tiller.

"On *planted,*" said the man in the apron. "Not a breath sooner. I'll not swing a bar through a girl."

Kiva walked to the hole.

The iron lid lay half across it. The arm went down through the foot of dark that was left, at the lane side of the post, humming. She stood on the lid. It rocked a little under her boots, and steadied. It had been under her cross for longer than anybody had kept the reason.

"The step first," said Orla.

"I know."

She had not chosen her ground. She knew which way the thing would throw her. It had its whole weight to the east of the post, where the black gate lay beyond the gap, and ten grey shapes on a hill beyond that. A ward under it would be flung along that line, back toward the gap, as a cork goes down a stream. She had gone with the haul in the Pit, and it had served.

Four paces east of the post was packed dirt, under the wall. She stepped off the lid, and went to it, and laid two fingers of her right hand on her breastbone.

"Cold."

She opened the still door.

She set it by its corners, wide, as she had learned to over a cistern. It went down through the dirt.

There was a room underneath.

It was not near. It was eight feet down, and bricked, and full of something that moved. Her stance found the top of it and rang, faintly, as a foot rings on a cellar flap. And through the brick came the count, slow and even, and it wanted her to keep it.

She shut the door. She had been open for less than a breath.

"That's not clean," said Orla.

"No. It's the main. It comes in under the gate and along the wall to the post. I felt it on Sunday, on my knees. I'd have put the arm down on top of the drain." She was cold all over. "It'd have gone straight back in."

"Then there's no go."

"There's no go *there.*"

She went back to the post. She stood with her hand on the iron band, and made herself think it through like a sum, and not like a fright.

The main came in from the east. It went out to the west, along the south row, toward the sheds. And from the north-east, slantwise, a small mouth came in from the core house. That was three roads into one hole. There was a fourth side.

She walked four paces north from the lid, out into the middle of the lane, between the two rows. She opened the door again.

It went down through packed dirt, and clay under the dirt, and it found nothing at all. There was only ground. It took her as a wharf takes a mooring.

"That's clean," said Orla. Her palm was flat on the dirt beside Kiva's boot. "That's as dead as a doorstep."

"Then that's the mark." Kiva shut the door, and scraped a cross with her heel. Her heart was going very fast. "North. Across the lane. Not along it." She raised her voice. She pitched it for the gap, and the shed, and a square shape standing east of the post where the Pit had taught him to stand. "*The mark's turned! It isn't east! East's the drain! I'm carrying it north, to here! Brask! Wace! Say it back!*"

"North," said Lowen Brask. He was already walking. "Not along. To the cross."

"North," said Wace. "To the cross. Moss, with me."

"It'll throw you east," said Rhea Sorn. She had not gone to her knot. She stood at the low wall, with her blade arm down. "I watched the Pit. You went where it sent you."

"I bent the ward to send it. It's a spoon. If I curve it the other way, it'll turn the shove a quarter round." She did not know that. She had done it with a sandbag, from the left. "I think it will."

"*Think,*" said Rhea.

"I can't weigh it."

"No." The white-streaked head went down, a half inch, and came up. "Nor could I have. Go on."

There was a wall of frost to be thought of, and nobody had thought of it.

She saw it then. If the corner went north, the cord would run from a gateway to a cross in the lane, and the hem would hang from it. It would cut the Old Yard slantwise, from the low wall to her heel. The post would be on the far side.

"The bar's behind it," she said. "When I've planted. The hem comes across between the cross and the post. Whoever's at the bar is shut in against the wall, and can't come to me. Whoever has the corner mustn't be at the bar."

Brask had stopped.

"She's right," he said. "I'd have been turning a screw and looked up to find a wall between me and my own job." He looked at the four fitters. "Can you finish it? The two turns?"

"We've had the best part of two days to finish it," said the man in the apron. He did not sound proud. "I'd not wager a girl's arm."

"Then it's called," said Kiva. "Brask and Cade to the bar, now, south of where the hem will be. Wace and Moss to the cross. *Two to the bar.* I want it said back."

"Two to the bar," said Perrin. He had come up beside Brask. His big face was grey. "Cade and Brask. I keep the word from there. And I'd like it known—"

"It's known," said Kiva.

"I hadn't said what."

"I know what."

He nodded. He went and put his hands on the ash pole, and the fitters gave it to him as men give up a thing that has beaten them.

She went back to the lid.

---

"*Out,*" said Selka Dray.

Her voice was not strong. It was exact. It came across five paces of dark from a chair against a post, and it did not hurry.

"Two. Three. Four. *In.* Two. Three—"

Kiva put up her hands.

They came up low, at her right hip, over the foot of dark beside the iron. Two fingers stood clear of the rest, straight and stiff. She bent the ward into the bowl of a spoon, and she bent it the wrong way round, with the curve to the east and the open side north.

It was cold. She could feel the cold through the silver.

"Hand, to Ember, to still. Rowan, changing on three, and on five."

That count was the go's, and hers. The fours were the arm's, and Dray's.

"*Out.* Two. Three. Four."

The arm drew up. The lid shivered under her boots.

"*In*—"

It was a hair early. It had been promised so.

"*One,*" said Kiva, and slid the pan under.

For four counts it lay there. It was the whole arm of a thing that stood ten abreast down a hill, and it lay in a tray between her palms, light as a clothesline, humming.

"*Two.*"

"*Out,*" said Dray.

They hauled.

It was not three posts. It was ten. The cord came up out of the dark like a hawser taking a ship, and found a spoon under it, and the whole of that haul went round the curve of her ward and into her right side.

It did not throw her east.

It turned. She felt it turn, a quarter round, as water turns in the bowl of a ladle, and it struck her in the ribs and the hip and pointed her out across the lane.

And it had a count in it. It was slow and even and many, and it was stronger than anything that she had ever had her hands on, and for the length of one tap it nearly had her.

"*Three.*"

She folded. Far edge. Near edge. A penny, in her palms. Shut.

Nobody.

She was nobody, going sideways over the packed dirt of the Old Yard with a hill's worth of haul in her spine. The ash went out of her like a breath. And the count went with it. It was left on the far side of a shut door, as mud is left on a step, exactly as it had been once before.

The struts caught her. Low, and long, and north. She was over the lane. Her left hand was cold to the wrist already, and she shut the Ember at the top and did not listen for it. She waited for the last thread to run home along her ribs and arrive.

"*Four.*"

Nobody, again.

It came out of a place that had been emptied in the Pit, and had only half filled. She had thought that she knew what that was like. There was a moment in the air when it seemed to her quite reasonable that there should be nothing to pay it with. Something paid. It went out through her left arm. She felt it pass the wrist, and go on, and she felt it pass the elbow.

*Arrive before you're there.*

She set the pan. Wide. By its corners.

"*Five!*"

Her right foot came down on a cross in the dirt, and the yard took her.

Then the corner came.

It came in at her heel like a tide coming up a slip. It was ten things leaning back on a line, and they went on leaning. It went down through her and into the clay and spread there, and the whole of the old ground under the wall took the weight of it, and held.

There was a white cord running down to the dirt a foot from her right boot. It was as taut as a stay. It ran away from her, east and a little south, over a low wall, to a black gateway, and from it a wall of frost was already dropping, thin as breath.

"*Planted!*" said Kiva. "*Six!*"

"*PLANTED!*" It was a boy's voice, from the lodge door, and it would have carried to the Sag. "*Planted, planted, send it!*"

"*Bar!*" That was Brask.

She did not look. She was counting.

She counted on from her own six, to make a five of it and not a four. *Seven. Eight. Nine.* The thing had fours. It bit on the first of each, as a girl in a chair had said that it would, and she went past it, a beat on, every time. It was like walking down a moving stair.

"Wace," she said. It came out between nine and ten. "The plank. Your end."

*Boom.*

A still door opened on her left, a yard off, and the lane rang with it. It was a great deal wider than hers. Wace had been a post before.

*Boom.* Another, beyond him. Hendry Moss stood there with his thick neck sunk between his shoulders, and between the two of them something hummed, like a plank laid from trestle to trestle.

"Mine," said Wace.

She leaned it.

She had never done that. She had watched it done to her, on a floor that boomed, by a boy who pushed one man and moved another. She let the corner go over, a little at a time, from her own heel to the end of that plank, as one lets a full tin down onto a shelf.

It went. She felt it go off her. She felt two still-doors take it and sink a finger's breadth into the dirt.

"It's got a count," she said. "Don't keep it. Go in fives. I'll call them. *One. Two. Three. Four. Five.*"

"One," said Wace, through his teeth. "Two."

She shut the door.

She shut it on an empty doorway. She was standing on a cross in the Old Yard with nothing open, and a cord a foot from her boot that was not hers any longer.

She did not sit down. She had meant to. She had two people beside her now, and then three; a long incurious boy in black and gold had walked out of the dark without a word and set his feet at Moss's shoulder, and the plank had become a thing with three ends.

"Yarrow," he said. "Halden. I'm told it goes round."

"*One,*" said Kiva. "*Two. Three. Four. Five.*"

Five paces off, a girl in a chair was calling fours.

---

Perrin Cade had the end of an ash pole in both hands, and he had never wanted so much to say that he could not.

It was not heavy. That was the insult of it. He had held three hundred and eighty kilos over his head for thirty counts on Thursday, and a man had cut the figure in stone. This was a bar as thick as his wrist, stuck through a post, and it would not go round.

"It's not weight," said Brask. He was at the other end, with his feet set. "It's a screw. You can't lift a screw. You lean, and you wait for it."

"I've leaned."

"You've pushed. Open the door, Cade. Wide. And don't push at all."

Perrin opened it.

The dirt of the Old Yard hummed under him. It was a narrow place to stand. There was thirty feet of brick a yard behind his heels, and in front of him, three paces off, a wall of frost had come down across the lane like a sheet let fall from a line. He could see through it. There were shapes on the far side, and a white cord, and a small figure with one arm hanging.

*You don't look up.*

He looked at the bar.

"On my word," said Brask. "*Give.*"

He let his weight go into the pole. He did not shove. He stood there, wide, and let the screw have the whole of him, as he had let the Long Terrace have a plate.

Something groaned under the ground.

"*Take.*"

The bar moved a hand.

"*Give.* It's coming. Don't chase it. *Take.*"

They walked it. It was the slowest walk of his life. A quarter turn, with the pole coming round toward the wall, and his knuckles a foot from the brick. A half. The post creaked in its iron bands. Down the half-covered hole between them there was a sound like a ship's timbers.

His ribs were telling him something. They had been strapped from the armpits to the hips for a spring and a summer, and they had a good memory.

*That's not pain,* he thought. *That's only where pain was.*

"Eight," said the man in the apron, behind him. He was counting on his fingers like a child. "That's eight. One more, and it's in the groove."

"*Give.*"

It stopped.

It stopped as it had stopped for four fitters since Tuesday. The pole stood across the lane, and the two of them stood at its ends with everything they had in the ground, and nothing moved.

"There's a hand's breadth of silt in the west groove," said Perrin. His voice came out squeezed. "The boy said. He was on it when I had him out."

"Then it's a hand's breadth of silt against two still-doors," said Brask. "I'd back us. *Take.*"

It did not take.

Through the frost he could hear two counts going. There was a girl calling fours, thin and exact. There was another calling fives, and her voice was not steady, and she had two people answering her now, and then three.

She had planted it and given it away. He had heard her do that. She had stood on that lid and named every part that was somebody else's, and his was this.

"Brask," he said. "I'd like it known that I mind."

"What?"

"All of it. Everything since the fifth month. I've been saying that I don't, since Thursday, and it was a lie; I was only pleased." He shut his eyes. "I mind that I had my back to the Pit. I mind that I carried an old man past Pryce holding a string. I mind that there's a wall of ice between me and my own team and I'm pushing a stick."

"Then mind it into the bar," said Lowen Brask, "and stop telling me."

He did.

It was not strength. He had no more of that. It was only that he stopped keeping any of himself back for afterward. The still door went down through the dirt, and the clay, and the old brick of the wall's footing, and the whole of the bottom of the School came up under his boots.

The screw shrieked.

The bar went round. It went a quarter, all at once, and the two of them walked after it, stumbling, and under the ground an iron plate as broad as a door went down into its groove and struck bottom with a sound like a struck tank.

"*Nine!*" said the man in the apron. "That's nine! She's seated!"

"*DOWN!*" Perrin had a voice like a tram, and he had been saving it. "*Gate's down! Kiva! It's shut!*"

He let go of the pole. His hands would not open at first.

He found that he was sitting on the dirt with his back against thirty feet of brick. There was a line of something warm down his side, under the tunic, where two ribs had been.

"That's the strapping," he said, to nobody. "I'd forgotten that I'd left it off."

"Did it go?" said Brask.

"No. It's only telling me."

Through the frost, a girl was still calling fives.

---

Mara Rowan had never liked a room with twenty-four of anything in it. It was too many to keep in one's head and too few to stop trying.

She had them, all the same. She had got the last of her hands at six minutes to twelve, off a tram, in a body: five students in Wren House green who had stood a Bench in the ninth month and had been waiting at the Brickfields shed with the carts. There was an operator to every cell that she could find one for. There was a student at the rest, with a red handle under one hand and a fresh can at his feet.

Rusk had cell one. He had taken his coat off.

"I've not pulled a handle since I was made examiner," he said. "It seems I remember where it is."

The three pumps stood at the far end, turning. Old Lumb, the keeper, sat on an upturned bucket beside the sump hatch with a watch on his knee and a notched stick down the hole.

"Five-and-twenty minutes," he said. "From the stroke that they stop. I've told you that four times. It rained Sunday. At five-and-twenty the sump's at its sill, and then it's the Hythe's cellars, and I'll have a hundred people at my door in the morning with wet coal."

"I heard you the first time, Mr. Lumb."

"I'm only saying."

She had a chalk mark on the sump wall. It was a hand above the water. The water came up to it, and fell.

It had come up to it for nineteen days.

There was a sounder on the keeper's desk, a small brass arm on a spring. A Works clerk sat over it with his pencil. Beside him stood a narrow woman with a grader's seal on a cord and a brass glass like a clock with no hands; and behind her, with his hat in his hand, a pleasant man in a black coat who had asked very civilly whether he might watch.

The sounder chattered.

"*Planted,*" said the clerk.

Mara did not allow herself to think about what that word had cost, or whom. She had told a girl in a yard that she could only manage it from two miles off.

"Mr. Lumb. Stop your pumps."

He threw three levers, one after another. The slow breathing at the end of the room faltered, and ran down, and ceased.

It was very quiet. She had not heard that room quiet in her life.

"Handles!" said Mara. "Hands on. On my word, and not on your neighbor's. *Quench!*"

Twenty-four red handles came over in one flat sound, like a shutter.

All down the long wall the needles stopped nodding. They did not go one after another. They went as they had lived for nineteen days, together, and lay against their pins.

"Dead," said Rusk. "That's a bank that can't be cooked. Whatever comes down that drain now, there's nothing here for it to foul."

"There's the water," said Mara.

She went to the sump hatch. She knelt, in her good skirt, and held the lamp down.

It came up to the mark. It fell. It came up again, a little short.

"It's still moving."

"It's less," said Lumb. He had his stick in. "A finger. It was two."

"It's being let go of. Something's had hold of it, and it's been taken off, and the water's only rocking with what was in it." She did not take her eyes from the chalk. "I want it flat. I'll not fill over a rock."

"Three minutes," said Lumb. "Of five-and-twenty."

The sounder went again.

"*Down,*" said the clerk.

That was a gate. That was an iron plate in a groove at the head of a hill, and a core house on the dry side of it. She had drawn that in ink on Tuesday and had not believed in it until this breath.

She watched the water.

It rose toward the mark, and did not reach it. It sank. It rose less. It was like watching a cup that somebody had set down too hard. She counted a slow twenty, and at the end of the twenty the black surface lay in the lamplight as flat as a slate, with her own face in it.

"It's lying," she said. Her voice was not quite her own. "Send that. One word. *Still.*"

The clerk's hand rattled on the key.

"Five minutes," said Lumb.

It was a long way to send a word, and a long way back. She knelt at the hatch and watched her own face in a drain. She had no notion what was being done on a hill by a School. She had arranged it so. She had told herself that it was so that she could do her work.

The sounder spoke.

"*Back,*" said the clerk. And then, since it went on: "*Hem down. Ten cut. It is out. Fill. H.V.*"

"*Fill!*" said Mara.

She was on her feet. She did not remember getting up.

"Draw your dead cans! Fresh home! Every one's had its pinch; I salted them myself. Count your sixty aloud, each of you, and I don't care that it sounds like a schoolroom. Then tune. And *not* to the lamp tune. Every one of you has a card under your handle with a number on it. That's your count. There aren't two alike in this room."

They drew. They filled. The long room filled with voices counting sixty, all out of time with one another, and it was the most beautiful noise that she had ever heard in a pump house.

She went down the row.

She had known that she would have to. Twenty-four fresh cells stood side by side on one wall, and a cell was a patient thing, and it leaned to its neighbor. Somebody had to stand between each and the next, for a slow ten, while its tune took hold, and feel which way it was leaning, and tell the tuner.

She laid her left palm on the first drum.

"That's yours, Mr. Rusk. Three. It's holding three. Good."

The second. "Five. It's leaning down toward three. Bring it up. More. There."

It was like standing in a doorway between two rooms of people who each wanted her to come in. She held her hand on the iron and counted against both. She had watched a girl do that on the cinders of a pit, in fives, and had not understood until now what it had been taking out of her.

"Seven. Good. Four. Nine."

"That one's going back," said the narrow woman at the glass. She had not raised her voice. "Eleventh from the door. It's fallen in with the tenth. I have it on the dial."

"I felt it. Quench eleven. Fill it again." Mara did not stop. "There's a second can under your stool, boy. That's what it's for."

"Eleven minutes," said Lumb.

At the fourteenth cell she noticed that her fingertips were white.

At the eighteenth she could not feel the iron. She could feel that there was a drum. She had a girl at home who used those words.

*The count of a thing. Somebody else has that.* Rusk had told her that it was all over the Works. He had thought it rather funny.

"Mr. Rusk," she said. "You'll take the odd ones from here. Your thumbs are as good as my palm; I've said otherwise for eleven years out of pride. I'll take the even."

He came without a word.

"Lumb. The first pump. It's on cells one to eight, and no others. Start it."

One pump drew, and let go, in the quiet. It had a slow, private, limping sound. It was a three.

"Fourteen minutes," said the keeper. "That's one. I want three."

The second took its eight at nineteen minutes, on a seven. The third would not. The eleventh cell had fallen in a second time, and a third, and it was a white-faced Wren House student who said at last, in a very small voice, that he thought the can was cut.

It was. It was civic stock, and somebody had put soot in it.

"Twenty-five," said Lumb. "That's my sill. There's water going over it. I can hear it."

"I can hear it too. Give me a can off the spare shelf. Not that one. The one with the wax unbroken." She had her left hand on the drum. She could not feel it. She watched the needle instead, as she had taught a child to do. "Sixty. Tune it to eleven. Hold it. *Hold.*"

It held.

"Start your pump, Mr. Lumb."

The third pump drew. It was a five.

Three pumps took their strokes in the long brick room, each in its own time, like three people walking who do not know one another. It was six-and-twenty minutes by an old man's watch. Along the left wall four-and-twenty needles trembled, and stood, and not one of them nodded to the next.

"Seven cellars," said Lumb. He had his ear to the hatch. "Perhaps eight. A hand of water in each. I've known the Hythe have worse from a blocked gutter." He looked at her. "You're holding that arm oddly, missus."

She was. She had it against her chest, with the right hand over it.

It was white to the wrist. It did not hurt. It lay there like a thing that she was minding for somebody.

"That's starved," said the woman with the glass. She had come round the desk. She took the hand, quite briskly, and turned it to the lamp. "I've seen it on graders who hold a reed too long over a loud tin. It's not burned. It wants ward-trace, plain, inside the hour, or it'll sulk for a month." She frowned. "The shelf's pinches were plain of everything, ward and all, and a hand can't feed on that. There's not a pinch of plain ward-trace in Meridian. It's all had the hair in it since the thaw."

"Then it will have to sulk," said Mara.

She was looking at the needles.

"I said to a watch officer that everything in my house had a meter and a way to shut it down," she said. "I've been nineteen days finding the second half." She was very tired. "I should like somebody to tell me what it cost."

Nobody in the pump house could.

---

"*Back!*" said Kiva.

She said it on Dray's *in.* She had waited for it. A boy had shouted *still* from the lodge door, and a watch officer had said, quite quietly, *Rowan, it's your word,* and she had let one whole slack go by to be certain of the next.

"*Give it to the ground!*"

Three still-doors let go together. The lane boomed.

There was light.
